<!-- theorem-1: body_md -->
\label{thm:fast-sampler}
    There is a universal constant $C > 0$ and a randomized sampling algorithm $\ensuremath{\mathsf{ALG}}$ with the following properties.
    Fix an integer $k \ge 2^{20}$ and a real density $\alpha > 0$ with
    \begin{align*}
        {\alpha \le \frac{2^k}{k^{16}}}.
    \end{align*}
    For sufficiently large $n$, with high probability over the random $k$-SAT formula $\Phi \sim \Phi(n, m, k)$ with $m = \floor{\alpha n}$, for every $0 < \varepsilon < 1$, $\ensuremath{\mathsf{ALG}}$ returns a satisfying assignment $X$ in time $T$ almost surely with
    \begin{align*}
        \ab\|\ensuremath{\mathsf{Law}}(X) - \mu_\Phi\|_{\mathrm{TV}} \le \varepsilon, \quad  
    \ensuremath{\mathbb{E}\ifblank{T}{}{_{T}}\left[#2\right]} 
 \le \ab(\frac{nk(\alpha+1)}{\varepsilon})^C.
    \end{align*}
<!-- end theorem-1 -->
<!-- remark-1: body_md -->
The expected-time guarantee applies to good formulas and averages over the algorithm's randomness. It is easy to give an explicit bounded-time version of \Cref{thm:fast-sampler}, following Markov's inequality. For example, by running the procedure of \Cref{thm:fast-sampler} with accuracy \(\varepsilon/2\) and returning an arbitrary assignment if its running time exceeds
    \[
        T_{\max}=\left\lceil
        \frac{2}{\varepsilon}
        \left(\frac{2nk(\alpha+1)}{\varepsilon}\right)^{C}
        \right\rceil,
    \]
    this gives a deterministic polynomial time cap on every input, while the output law is still within total variation distance \(\varepsilon\) of \(\mu_\Phi\).
<!-- end remark-1 -->
<!-- remark-2: body_md -->
The cutoff \(2^{20}\), the power \(16\), and the running-time degree are conservative. We have not optimized these constants as much as possible.
<!-- end remark-2 -->
<!-- theorem-2: body_md -->
\label{thm:kotecky-preiss}
Let $u_\gamma\ge0$ satisfy
$|w_\gamma|\le u_\gamma$ for every $\gamma\in\mathcal P$, and let
$A:\mathcal P\to[0,\infty)$ be a penalty function. Suppose that
\begin{equation}
 \forall\,\gamma\in\mathcal P,\qquad
 \sum_{\gamma'\not\sim\gamma}
       u_{\gamma'}e^{A\left(\gamma'\right)}\le A(\gamma).
 \label{eq:kotecky-preiss-condition}
\end{equation}
Then the series $\mathcal L(w)$ in
\eqref{eq:polymer-cluster-expansion-prelim} converges absolutely and
\[
 \mathcal L(w)=\log\Xi(w).
\]
Here $\log\Xi(w)$ denotes the analytic branch satisfying
$\log\Xi(0)=0$.
Moreover, for every fixed polymer $\gamma_0$,
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{\ell\ge1}\frac1{(\ell-1)!}
 \sum_{\gamma_2,\ldots,\gamma_\ell\in\mathcal P}
 \left|\varphi\left(\gamma_0,\gamma_2,\ldots,\gamma_\ell\right)\right|
 \prod_{i=2}^{\ell}u_{\gamma_i}
 \le e^{A\left(\gamma_0\right)}.
 \label{eq:kotecky-preiss-pinned}
\end{equation}
For $\ell=1$, the inner tuple and product in
\eqref{eq:kotecky-preiss-pinned} are empty, and their contribution is
one.
<!-- end theorem-2 -->
<!-- theorem-3: body_md -->
\label{thm:aymmetric-local-lemma}
    Given bad events $B_1, \ldots, B_m$ with dependency neighborhoods $N(1), \ldots, N(m)$, if there exist $\left\{x_a \in (0, 1)\right\}_{a = 1}^m$ such that
    \begin{align*}
        \forall a = 1, \ldots, m, \quad  
    \ensuremath{\mathbb{P}\ifblank{B_a}{}{_{B_a}}\left(#2\right)} 
 \le x_a \prod_{c \in N(a)} \left(1 - x_c\right)
    \end{align*}
    Then the probability avoiding all bad events is at least $\prod_{a = 1}^m \left(1 - x_a\right) > 0$.
    Moreover,for any event $\mathcal E$, it holds that
    \begin{align*}
         
    \ensuremath{\mathbb{P}\ifblank{\mathcal E \mid \bigwedge_{a = 1}^m \overline{B_a}}{}{_{\mathcal E \mid \bigwedge_{a = 1}^m \overline{B_a}}}\left(#2\right)} 
 \le  
    \ensuremath{\mathbb{P}\ifblank{\mathcal E}{}{_{\mathcal E}}\left(#2\right)} 
 \prod_{a \in N(\mathcal E)} \left(1 - x_a\right)
    \end{align*}
    where $N(\mathcal E) \triangleq \left\{a \in [m] \mid \ensuremath{\operatorname{vbl}}(\mathcal E) \cap \ensuremath{\operatorname{vbl}}\left(B_a\right) \neq \emptyset\right\}$.
<!-- end theorem-3 -->
<!-- lemma-1: body_md -->
\label{lem:efron-stein}
Let $r\ge1$ and let $Z_1,\ldots,Z_r$ be independent random variables,
where $Z_i$ takes values in a finite set $\mathcal S_i$.
Write $Z=\left(Z_1,\ldots,Z_r\right)$ and, for each $i\in[r]$, let
$Z_{-i}=\left(Z_j\right)_{j\in[r]\setminus\{i\}}$ denote all coordinates except $i$.
Then, for every $h:\prod_{i=1}^r\mathcal S_i\to\mathbb R$,
\begin{equation}
 \operatorname{Var}\left(h(Z)\right)
 \le\sum_{i=1}^r\mathbb E\!\left[
       \operatorname{Var}\left(h(Z)\mid Z_{-i}\right)\right].
 \label{eq:efron-stein-conditional-variance}
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-1 -->
<!-- lemma-2: body_md -->
\label{lem:expansion}
    Suppose that $k \ge 30$ and $n^2 \ge 2n_0$.
    With probability at least $1 - \frac{2n_0}{n^2} - \frac{4}{3} \cdot 4^{-n^{1/3}}$, for every set $S$ of at most $r$ clauses, it holds that
    \begin{align} \label{eq:small-set-expansion}
        \ab|\ensuremath{\operatorname{vbl}}(S)| \ge (k - 4) \ab|S|.
    \end{align}
<!-- end lemma-2 -->
<!-- proof-1: body_md -->
Fix an integer $t \le r$.
    Assume that there exists a subset $S$ of exactly $t$ clauses such that
    \begin{align*}
        \ab|\ensuremath{\operatorname{vbl}}(S)| < (k - 4) \ab|S| = (k - 4)t.
    \end{align*}
    Since $(k - 4)t \le (k - 4) q_0 n < n$ by \eqref{eq:parameter-choice}, it holds that all variables in $S$ completely lie in some $U \subseteq [n]$ of size $(k - 4)t$.

    For a fixed subset $S$ of clause labels with $\ab|S| = t$ and a variable subset $U$ of size $(k - 4)t$, we bound the probability that all variables in $S$ lie in $U$ as
    \begin{align*}
         
    \ensuremath{\mathbb{P}\ifblank{\ensuremath{\operatorname{vbl}}(S) \subseteq U}{}{_{\ensuremath{\operatorname{vbl}}(S) \subseteq U}}\left(#2\right)} 
 \le \ab(\frac{(k - 4)t}{n})^{k t}.
    \end{align*}
    By the union bound together with $\binom{n}{k} \le (\mathrm{e} n/k)^k$,
    \begin{align*}
         
    \ensuremath{\mathbb{P}\ifblank{\exists S,\; \ab|S| = t,\; \ab|\ensuremath{\operatorname{vbl}}(S)| < (k - 4) \ab|S|}{}{_{\exists S,\; \ab|S| = t,\; \ab|\ensuremath{\operatorname{vbl}}(S)| < (k - 4) \ab|S|}}\left(#2\right)} 
 &\le \binom{m}{t} \binom{n}{(k - 4)t} \ab(\frac{(k - 4)t}{n})^{kt} \\
        &\le \ab(\frac{\mathrm{e} m}{t})^t \ab(\frac{\mathrm{e} n}{(k - 4)t})^{(k - 4)t} \frac{(k - 4)^{kt}t^{kt}}{n^{kt}} \\
        &\le \ab(\mathrm{e}^{k - 3} \cdot \frac{m}{n}(k - 4)^4 \ab(\frac{t}{n})^3)^t \\
        &\le \ab(n_0 \cdot \frac{t^3}{n^3})^t.
    \end{align*}
    For $t \le n^{1/3}$, $n_0 t^3/n^3 \le n_0/n^2 \le 1/2$.
    Then
    \begin{align*}
        \sum_{t = 1}^{n^{1/3}} \ab(n_0 \cdot \frac{t^3}{n^3})^t \le \frac{n_0/n^2}{1 - n_0/n^2} \le \frac{2n_0}{n^2}.
    \end{align*}
    Otherwise, using $t/n \le q_0$ and $n_0 \cdot q_0^3 = 1/4$,
    \begin{align*}
        \sum_{t = n^{1/3}}^n \ab(n_0 \cdot \frac{t^3}{n^3})^t \le \sum_{t > n^{1/3}} 4^{-t} \le \frac{4}{3} \cdot 4^{-n^{1/3}}.
    \end{align*}
    Thus the two cases together conclude the lemma.
<!-- end proof-1 -->
<!-- lemma-3: body_md -->
\label{lem:incident-clause-estimate}
    For every $k \ge 2$ and $m \ge 0$, with probability at least $1 - n^{-3}$, every connected variable subset $U$ satisfies
    \begin{align*}
        I(U) < C_{\mathrm{INC}} \ab|U| + 4\ln n.
    \end{align*}
<!-- end lemma-3 -->
<!-- proof-2: body_md -->
At first, we consider the case $\ab|U| > 1$.
    For a connected variable subset $U$, pick a spanning tree of $\mathcal{G}[U]$.
    For every edge in this spanning tree, select a clause containing both endpoints of it.
    After discarding repeated clause labels, we obtain a clause certificate $T$ such that
    \begin{align*}
        \ab|T| \le \ab|U| - 1, \quad U \subseteq \ensuremath{\operatorname{vbl}}(T).
    \end{align*}
    Moreover, $T$ is connected in $\mathcal{H}$.
    
    Next, we count the number of connected clauses.
    Let $N_t$ be the number of connected $t$-clause labels.
    For a collection of $t$ clause labels, if they are connected, there is a spanning tree of size $t$.
    Root the tree at an arbitrary label.
    We reveal such a tree in the order that the parent precedes its children.
    Then conditional on everything already revealed, the probability that a new clause intersects its parent is at most $k \cdot k/n = k^2/n$.
    Consequently, by the Cayley's formula,
    \begin{align*}
         
    \ensuremath{\mathbb{E}\ifblank{N_t}{}{_{N_t}}\left[#2\right]} 
 &\le \binom{m}{t} t^{t - 2} \ab(\frac{k^2}{n})^{t - 1} \\
        &\le \frac{\mathrm{e}^t m^t}{t^t} \cdot t^{t - 2} \cdot \frac{k^{2(t - 1)}}{n^{t - 1}} \\
        &\le \frac{n}{k^2 t^2} \ab(\mathrm{e} \alpha k^2)^t \\
        &\le n \left(\mathrm{e} \lambda k^2\right)^t
    \end{align*}

    Now we expose a certificate $T$ and count the remaining incidences.
    For a variable subset $U$ whose certificate is $T$, let $X$ be the number of remaining unexposed clauses which intersect $U$.
    Conditional on the reveal of $T$, $X$ follows a binomial distribution, \emph{i.e.}\xspace,
    \begin{align*}
        X \sim \mathrm{Bin}\ab(m - \ab|T|, p) \quad \text{where}\; p = 1 - \ab(1 - \frac{\ab|U|}{n})^k \le \frac{k\ab|U|}{n}.
    \end{align*}
    Since $\ab|T| \le \ab|U| - 1$, the event $\left\{I(U) \ge C_{\mathrm{INC}} \ab|U| + 4\ln n\right\}$ implies
    \begin{align*}
        X \ge \left(C_{\mathrm{INC}} - 1\right) \ab|U| + 4\ln n.
    \end{align*}
    Note that $\left(C_{\mathrm{INC}} - 1\right) \ab|U| \ge \mathrm{e}^2 \cdot  
    \ensuremath{\mathbb{E}\ifblank{X \mid T}{}{_{X \mid T}}\left[#2\right]} 
$ since $ 
    \ensuremath{\mathbb{E}\ifblank{X \mid T}{}{_{X \mid T}}\left[#2\right]} 
 \le m \cdot k\ab|U|/n \le \alpha k \ab|U|$.
    By the Chernoff bound,
    \begin{align*}
         
    \ensuremath{\mathbb{P}\ifblank{X \ge \left(C_{\mathrm{INC}} - 1\right)\ab|U| + 4\ln n \mid T}{}{_{X \ge \left(C_{\mathrm{INC}} - 1\right)\ab|U| + 4\ln n \mid T}}\left(#2\right)} 
 \le \mathrm{e}^{-\left(\left(C_{\mathrm{INC}} - 1\right) \ab|U| + 4\ln n\right)}.
    \end{align*}
    Set the event
    \begin{align*}
        \mathcal{E}_{\ell} \triangleq \left\{\exists U,\; \ab|U| = \ell \land I(U) \ge \left(C_{\mathrm{INC}} - 1\right) \ab|U| + 4\ln n\right\}.
    \end{align*}
    By the union bound,
    \begin{align*}
         
    \ensuremath{\mathbb{P}\ifblank{\mathcal{E}_{\ell}}{}{_{\mathcal{E}_{\ell}}}\left(#2\right)} 
 &\le \sum_{t = 1}^{\min\;\left\{\ell - 1, m\right\}}  
    \ensuremath{\mathbb{E}\ifblank{N_t}{}{_{N_t}}\left[#2\right]} 
 \binom{kt}{\ell} \mathrm{e}^{-\left(C_{\mathrm{INC}} - 1\right) \ell + 4\ln n} \\
        &\le \ell n \left(\mathrm{e} \lambda k^2\right)^\ell (\mathrm{e} k)^{\ell} \mathrm{e}^{-\left(C_{\mathrm{INC}} - 1\right) \ell - 4\ln n} \\
        &= n^{-3} \cdot \ell \exp\ab(-\ell\ab(C_{\mathrm{INC}} - 1 - \ln\left(\mathrm{e}^2 \lambda k^3\right))).
    \end{align*}
    By \eqref{eq:parameter-choice}, for $k \ge 2$ and $\lambda \ge 1$,
    \begin{align*}
        C_{\mathrm{INC}} - 1 - \ln\left(\mathrm{e}^2 \lambda k^3\right) \ge 100k^2 \lambda - 3 - 3\ln k - \ln \lambda \ge 2.
    \end{align*}
    Therefore,
    \begin{align*}
         
    \ensuremath{\mathbb{P}\ifblank{\bigcup_{\ell \ge 2} \mathcal{E}_{\ell}}{}{_{\bigcup_{\ell \ge 2} \mathcal{E}_{\ell}}}\left(#2\right)} 
 &\le n^{-3} \sum_{\ell \ge 2} \ell \cdot \mathrm{e}^{-2\ell} \\
        &= n^{-3} \cdot \frac{\mathrm{e}^{-4}\left(2 - \mathrm{e}^{-2}\right)}{\left(1 - \mathrm{e}^{-2}\right)^2} \\
        &\le \frac{1}{20} \cdot n^{-3}.
    \end{align*}

    To conclude the lemma, it remains to consider the case $\ab|U| = 1$, \emph{i.e.}\xspace, $U = \left\{v\right\}$.
    Observe that $I(U)$ is dominated by a binomial distribution $\mathrm{Bin}(m, k/n)$.
    By the Chernoff bound,
    \begin{align*}
         
    \ensuremath{\mathbb{P}\ifblank{I(U) \ge C_{\mathrm{INC}} + 4\ln n}{}{_{I(U) \ge C_{\mathrm{INC}} + 4\ln n}}\left(#2\right)} 
 \le n^{-4}\mathrm{e}^{-C_{\mathrm{INC}}}.
    \end{align*}
    By the union bound, the failure probability is at most
    \begin{align*}
        \frac{1}{20} \cdot n^{-3} + n \cdot \mathrm{e}^{-C_{\mathrm{INC}}} n^{-4} \le n^{-3}.
    \end{align*}
    Therefore we conclude the lemma.
<!-- end proof-2 -->
<!-- lemma-4: body_md -->
\label{lem:high-degree-tail-bound}
    For $k \ge 30$ and $h = \ceil{q_0 n}$, it holds that
    \begin{align*}
         
    \ensuremath{\mathbb{P}\ifblank{\ab|\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{high}}| \le r}{}{_{\ab|\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{high}}| \le r}}\left(#2\right)} 
 \ge 1 - \mathrm{e}^{-h}.
    \end{align*}
<!-- end lemma-4 -->
<!-- proof-3: body_md -->
It suffices to show that $ 
    \ensuremath{\mathbb{P}\ifblank{\ab|\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{high}}| \ge h}{}{_{\ab|\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{high}}| \ge h}}\left(#2\right)} 
 \le \mathrm{e}^{-h}$.
    Fix a variable subset $U$ of size $h$.
    Let $X_U$ be the random variable to denote the number of literals whose variable names are in $U$.
    Then $X_U$ is a binomial variable:
    \begin{align*}
        X_U \sim \mathrm{Bin}\ab(km, \frac{h}{n}) \quad \text{thus} \quad  
    \ensuremath{\mathbb{E}\ifblank{X_U}{}{_{X_U}}\left[#2\right]} 
 = \frac{m}{n}k h \le \lambda kh.
    \end{align*}
    If $U \subseteq \mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{high}}$, it must hold that $X_U \ge h \Delta$.
    The Chernoff bound provides that
    \begin{align*}
         
    \ensuremath{\mathbb{P}\ifblank{X \ge \Delta h}{}{_{X \ge \Delta h}}\left(#2\right)} 
 \le \ab(\frac{\mathrm{e} \lambda k h}{\Delta h})^{\Delta h} = \ab(\frac{\mathrm{e}}{6k^6})^{\Delta h}
    \end{align*}
    since $\Delta = 6k^7 \lambda$.
    By the union bound,
    \begin{align*}
         
    \ensuremath{\mathbb{P}\ifblank{\ab|\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{high}}| \ge h}{}{_{\ab|\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{high}}| \ge h}}\left(#2\right)} 
 &\le \binom{n}{h} \ab(\frac{\mathrm{e}}{6k^6})^{\Delta h} \\
        &\le \ab(\frac{\mathrm{e} n}{h})^h \ab(\frac{\mathrm{e}}{6k^6})^{\Delta h} \\
        &\le \ab(\frac{\mathrm{e}}{q_0} \ab(\frac{\mathrm{e}}{6k^6})^\Delta)^h.
    \end{align*}
    For $k \ge 30$, by choices of parameters \eqref{eq:parameter-choice},
    \begin{align*}
        \frac{\mathrm{e}}{q_0} \ab(\frac{\mathrm{e}}{6k^6})^{\Delta} \le \mathrm{e}^{-1}.
    \end{align*}
    Hence we conclude the lemma.
<!-- end proof-3 -->
<!-- lemma-5: body_md -->
\label{lem:bounded-core-growth}
    Assume $r \ge 1, \ab|\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{high}}| \le r$ and the expansion event of \Cref{lem:expansion}.
    For every $S \subseteq \mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{high}}$, it holds that
    \begin{align*}
        \ab|\ensuremath{\mathsf{Core}}(S)| \le \frac{k - 4}{5} \ab|S| \le \frac{k}{2} \ab|S|.
    \end{align*}
<!-- end lemma-5 -->
<!-- proof-4: body_md -->
By construction of $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Core}}(S)$, every selected clause has at most $k - 9$ new variables added to the core.
    Therefore, after any $t \le r$ selections, by \Cref{lem:expansion},
    \begin{align*}
        (k - 4)t \le \ab|\ensuremath{\operatorname{vbl}}(\text{selected clauses})| \le \ab|S| + (k - 9)t
    \end{align*}
    which implies that $5t \le \ab|S|$.
    It also implies that the process cannot reach $t = r$ otherwise it must hold $5r \le \ab|S| \le r$.
    Therefore
    \begin{align*}
        \ab|\ensuremath{\mathsf{Core}}(S)| \le \ab|S| + (k - 9)\frac{\ab|S|}{5} = \frac{k - 4}{5} \ab|S|,
    \end{align*}
    which concludes this lemma.
<!-- end proof-4 -->
<!-- lemma-6: body_md -->
\label{lem:component-growth-size}
    Assume the joint event of \Cref{lem:expansion,lem:incident-clause-estimate,lem:high-degree-tail-bound}.
    For any non-empty subset $J \subseteq [s]$ and $U = \bigcup_{i \in J} B_i$, it holds that
    \begin{align*}
        \ab|U| \le \frac{k}{2} \ab|U \cap \mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{high}}|.
    \end{align*}
    Moreover, each component is of size at most $\log_2 n$.
<!-- end lemma-6 -->
<!-- proof-5: body_md -->
The key ingredient to prove the first inequality is the following identity:
    \begin{align} \label{eq:component-identity}
        \ensuremath{\mathsf{Core}}\left(U \cap \mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{high}}\right) = U
    \end{align}

    To show $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Core}}\left(U \cap \mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{high}}\right) \subseteq U$, suppose that there is a clause containing at least $9$ variables in $U$.
    All its variables should be in $\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{bad}}$ by the construction of $\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{bad}}$ and they are connected in $\mathcal{G}[\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{bad}}]$.
    Note that each $B_i$ is a maximal connected component for $i = 1, \ldots, s$.
    These variables must lie in one component.
    Therefore they are in $U$ since the clause meets $U$.

    Now we prove that $U \subseteq \ensuremath{\mathsf{Core}}\left(U \cap \mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{high}}\right)$.
    Take a sequence of clause additions constructing $\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{bad}}$ from $\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{high}}$.
    Then we retain only the additions whose clauses lie in $U$.
    It is clear that this is a legal sequence to construct $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Core}}\left(U \cap \mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{high}}\right)$ from $U \cap \mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{high}}$, and the sequence finally produces all of $U$ since $U \subseteq \mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{bad}}$.
    Hence $U \subseteq \ensuremath{\mathsf{Core}}\left(U \cap \mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{high}}\right)$.

    Putting two directions together, we conclude \eqref{eq:component-identity}.
    By \Cref{lem:bounded-core-growth},
    \begin{align*}
        \ab|U| = \ab|\ensuremath{\mathsf{Core}}\left(U \cap \mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{high}}\right)| \le \frac{k}{2} \ab|U \cap \mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{high}}|
    \end{align*}
    which is exactly the first conclusion.

    To bound the size of each component, suppose that there is a component $B_i \in \mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{bad}}$ such that $\ab|B_i| > \log_2 n$.
    By the inequality above, it holds that
    \begin{align*}
        \ab|B_i| \le \frac{k}{2} \ab|B_i \cap \mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{high}}|.
    \end{align*}
    Consider the number of clauses incident to $B_i \cap \mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{high}}$.
    Since every variable in $B_i \cap \mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{high}}$ has degree at least $\Delta$,
    \begin{align*}
        \Delta \ab|B_i \cap \mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{high}}| \le \sum_{v \in B_i \cap \mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{high}}} \deg(v) \le k I\left(B_i\right).
    \end{align*}
    The last inequality holds since every clause incident to $B_i$ contains at most $k$ distinct variables in $B_i \cap \mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{high}}$.
    By \Cref{lem:incident-clause-estimate},
    \begin{align*}
        \frac{2\Delta \ab|B_i|}{k} \le \Delta \ab|B_i \cap \mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{high}}| \le k C_{\mathrm{INC}} \ab|B_i| + 4k \ln n.
    \end{align*}
    By the assumption $\ab|B_i| > \log_2 n$, $\ln n < \ab|B_i| \cdot \ln 2$.
    Then we have
    \begin{align*}
        \frac{2\Delta}{k} \ab|B_i| < k\left(C_{\mathrm{INC}} + 4\ln 2\right) \ab|B_i|
    \end{align*}
    giving that $1 < k^2\left(C_{\mathrm{INC}} + 4\ln 2\right)/(2\Delta)$.
    By \eqref{eq:parameter-choice},
    \begin{align*}
        \frac{k^2\left(C_{\mathrm{INC}} + 4\ln 2\right)}{2\Delta} = \frac{25}{3k^3} + \frac{\ln 2}{3k^5\lambda} < 1
    \end{align*}
    for $k \ge 30$ and $\lambda \ge 1$.
    This contradiction concludes that $\ab|B_i| \le \log_2 n$ for every $i = 1, \ldots, s$.
<!-- end proof-5 -->
<!-- proposition-1: body_md -->
\label{prop:small-core-clause-component}
    Assume the joint event of \Cref{lem:expansion,lem:incident-clause-estimate,lem:high-degree-tail-bound}.
    For every integer $L \in [\log_2{n}, r]$ and $i = 1, \ldots, s$, there are at most $L$ core clauses meeting $B_i$.
<!-- end proposition-1 -->
<!-- proof-6: body_md -->
Suppose that there exists $B_i$ containing at least $L$ core clauses.
    Select $L$ of them to form $S$.
    By \Cref{lem:expansion}, since $L \le r$, it holds that
    \begin{align*}
        \ab|\ensuremath{\operatorname{vbl}}(S)| \ge (k - 4)L \ge L.
    \end{align*}
    On the other hand, $\ensuremath{\operatorname{vbl}}(S)$ must be fully contained in $B_i$ by the assumption that $B_i$ is maximal.
    This leads to contradiction that $\ensuremath{\operatorname{vbl}}(S) \le \ab|B_i| < \log_2{n} \le L \le \ab|\ensuremath{\operatorname{vbl}}(S)|$.
<!-- end proof-6 -->
<!-- lemma-7: body_md -->
Assume the joint event of \Cref{lem:expansion,lem:incident-clause-estimate,lem:high-degree-tail-bound}.
    Then the core formula is satisfiable with any choice of literal signs, and its feasible set is
    \begin{align*}
        \Omega \triangleq \prod_{i = 1}^s \Omega_i \neq \emptyset
    \end{align*}
    where $\Omega_i$ consists of all feasible assignments to $B_i$ satisfying all core clauses meeting that component.
<!-- end lemma-7 -->
<!-- proof-7: body_md -->
For every $i = 1, \ldots, s$, let $\mathcal{C}_i$ be the collection of core clauses meeting $B_i$.
    Construct a bipartite graph with:
    \begin{itemize}
        \item one left vertex for each clause label $a \in \mathcal{C}_i$;
        \item one right vertex for each variable in $v \in B_i$;
        \item an edge $a \sim v$ if and only if $v \in \ensuremath{\operatorname{vbl}}(a)$.
    \end{itemize}
    For every $S \subseteq \mathcal{C}_i$, the neighbors of $S$ are exactly $\ensuremath{\operatorname{vbl}}(S)$.
    By \Cref{prop:small-core-clause-component}, for every subset $S \subseteq \mathcal{C}_i$, $\ab|S| \le \ab|\mathcal{C}_i| \le r$, thus $\ensuremath{\operatorname{vbl}}(S) \ge (k - 4) \ab|S|$.
    Thus by Hall's theorem, there is a injection $f_i: \mathcal{C}_i \to B_i$ such that $f_i(a)$ occurs in the clause $C_a$.
    
    Now we construct a satisfying assignment based on the matching.
    Given any choice of literal signs, for every clause $a \in \mathcal{C}_i$,
    \begin{itemize}
        \item choose one occurrence of $f_i(a)$ in $C_a$;
        \item set $f_i(a)$ making $C_a$ true.
    \end{itemize}
    Since $f_i$ is injective, different clauses prescribe values to different variables.
    Finally, we set all unmatched variables in $B_i$ arbitrarily.
    Hence we conclude that $\Omega_i \neq \emptyset$ for every $i = 1, \ldots, s$.
    
    Since $B_1, \ldots, B_s$ are disjoint and thus each core clause belongs to exactly one component, then it holds that every assignment in $\Omega \triangleq \prod_{i = 1}^s \Omega_i$ is feasible for the full core formula.
    On the other hand, given a feasible assignment of the core formula, its restriction to $B_i$ also satisfies $\mathcal{C}_i$ for each $i = 1, \ldots, s$, meaning that it belongs to $\Omega$.
    Thus we conclude that the feasible set of the core formula is exactly $\Omega$.
<!-- end proof-7 -->
<!-- proposition-2: body_md -->
\label{prop:good-property}
    Assume the joint event of \Cref{lem:expansion,lem:incident-clause-estimate,lem:high-degree-tail-bound}.
    The the following properties hold.
    \begin{itemize}
        \item The dependency graph $\mathcal{H}_{\Psi}$ of $\Psi$ has maximum degree $d \le k\Delta$ and thus $d + 1 \le \overline{D}$ by \eqref{eq:parameter-choice}.
        \item For every $S \subseteq \mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{good}}$ with $\ab|S| \le L$ where $L$ is an integer $L \in [\log_2, r]$,
        \begin{align*}
            \ab|\ensuremath{\operatorname{vbl}}^{\mathrm{good}}(S)| \ge (k - 12) \ab|S|.
        \end{align*}
    \end{itemize}
<!-- end proposition-2 -->
<!-- proof-8: body_md -->
Observe that $\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{high}} \subseteq \mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{bad}}$ and $\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{good}} = \mathcal{V} \setminus \mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{bad}}$.
    Then for every variable $v \in \mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{good}}$, it holds that $\deg(v) < \Delta$.
    Therefore the maximum degree of $\mathcal{H}_{\Psi}$ is at most $k\Delta$.

    For every $S \subseteq \mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{good}}$ with $\ab|S| \le L$, by \Cref{lem:expansion},
    \begin{align*}
        \ab|\ensuremath{\operatorname{vbl}}^{\mathrm{good}}(S)| &\ge \ab|\ensuremath{\operatorname{vbl}}(S)| - \sum_{i \in S} \ab|\ensuremath{\operatorname{vbl}}(i) \cap \mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{bad}}| \\
        &\ge (k - 4) \ab|S| - 8 \ab|S| \\
        &\ge (k - 12) \ab|S|
    \end{align*}
    which concludes the second property.
<!-- end proof-8 -->
<!-- lemma-8: body_md -->
\label{lem:core-assignment-good-variable-extension}
    Assume the joint event of \Cref{lem:expansion,lem:incident-clause-estimate,lem:high-degree-tail-bound}.
    Put $p_* = 2^{-k + 12}$ and $C_* = 10^4$.
    If
    \begin{align} \label{eq:good-variable-condition}
        p_* \overline{D} \le \frac{1}{2C_*},
    \end{align}
    then every deletion sub-formula $\Psi[F]$ of $\Psi$ is satisfiable and it holds that for every $a \notin F$,
    \begin{align} \label{eq:violating-upper-bound}
        \mu_F(E_a) \le \mathrm{e} p_*.
    \end{align}
    Consequently for $J \subseteq F$, $Z_{F \setminus J} \le (1 - \mathrm{e} p_*)^{-\ab|J|} Z_F$.
    
    Moreover, for every $\sigma \in \Omega$, it extends to a satisfying assignment of $\Phi$.
    Let $D_{\sigma}$ be the collection of labels whose bad-variable literals are all false at $\sigma$.
    Then it holds that
    \begin{align*}
        \mu_{\Phi}\ab(\cdot \mid \mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{bad}} \gets \sigma) = \mu_{\Psi[D_{\sigma}]}(\cdot).
    \end{align*}
<!-- end lemma-8 -->
<!-- proof-9: body_md -->
Fix a deletion sub-formula $\Psi[F]$.
    Every clause in it has violating probability at most $p_*$, and has at most $d + 1 \le \overline{D}$ dependencies by \Cref{prop:good-property}.
    Take $x_a = x \triangleq \mathrm{e} p_*$ in \Cref{thm:aymmetric-local-lemma} for every clause label $a \in \Psi[F]$.
    It holds that for every $a$, by $x \overline{D} \le \mathrm{e}/\left(2C_*\right) < 1/128$ and $x < 1/2$,
    \begin{align*}
        p_* = x \mathrm{e}^{-1} \le x (1 - x)^{d}.
    \end{align*}
    Therefore there exists a satisfying assignment for $\Psi[F]$.

    To show \eqref{eq:violating-upper-bound}, we prove it by induction on $\ab|F|$.
    First, since all deletion sub-formula $\Psi[F]$ is satisfiable, $Z_F > 0$.
    For $F = \emptyset$, it is clear to see $\mu_\emptyset(E_a) \le p_* \le \mathrm{e} p_*$.
    Now fix $F$ with $\ab|F| = t > 1$ and $a \notin F$.
    Split $F$ into the neighbors and non-neighbors of $a$:
    \begin{align*}
        S = F \cap N_{\mathcal{H}_\Psi}(a), \quad T = F \setminus S
    \end{align*}
    and order $S = \left\{b_1, \ldots, b_r\right\}$ where $r \le d$.
    
    The event $E_a$ uses variables disjoint from every clause in $T$.
    Hence
    \begin{align*}
        \nu\ab(E_a \mid \ensuremath{\mathsf{Good}}(F)) = \frac{\nu\ab(E_a \cap \ensuremath{\mathsf{Good}}(S) \mid \ensuremath{\mathsf{Good}}(T))}{\nu\ab(\ensuremath{\mathsf{Good}}(S) \mid \ensuremath{\mathsf{Good}}(T))} \le \frac{p_*}{\nu\ab(\ensuremath{\mathsf{Good}}(S) \mid \ensuremath{\mathsf{Good}}(T))}
    \end{align*}
    To bound the denominator, by the chain rule,
    \begin{align*}
        \nu\ab(\ensuremath{\mathsf{Good}}(S) \mid \ensuremath{\mathsf{Good}}(T)) = \prod_{j = 1}^r \ab(1 - \nu\ab(E_{b_j} \mid \ensuremath{\mathsf{Good}}(T \cup \left\{b_1, \ldots, b_{j - 1}\right\}))).
    \end{align*}
    By induction, each factor is at least $1 - \mathrm{e} p_*$.
    Therefore
    \begin{align*}
        \nu\ab(\ensuremath{\mathsf{Good}}(S) \mid \ensuremath{\mathsf{Good}}(T)) \ge (1 - \mathrm{e} p_*)^r \ge (1 - \mathrm{e} p_*)^d.
    \end{align*}
    Thus we conclude that $\nu\ab(E_a \mid \ensuremath{\mathsf{Good}}(F)) \le p_*/(1 - \mathrm{e} p_*)^d \le \mathrm{e} p_*$.

    To complete the proof, fix a partial assignment $\sigma \in \Omega$ on the core formula.
    A good formula either is already satisfied by $\sigma$ and disappears, or retains exactly its clause of $\Psi$.
    Then it is trivial to see the satisfiability under $\sigma$ and the identity of conditional law.
<!-- end proof-9 -->
<!-- theorem-4: body_md -->
\label{thm:modified-recursive-insertion}
    Suppose that there exist $K_0, K_2 \ge 0$ and $\kappa \in [0, 1)$ satisfying that for every non-empty subset $F \subseteq \mathcal{F}$ and $a \in F$,
    \begin{align}
        \sum_{S \in \ensuremath{\mathsf{CS}}_F(a)} q_S R_S^2 &\le K_0, \label{eq:recursive-insertion-1} \\
        \sum_{S \in \ensuremath{\mathsf{CS}}_F(a)} q_S R_S^2 \ell(S) &\le \kappa, \label{eq:recursive-insertion-2} \\
        \sum_{S, T \in \ensuremath{\mathsf{CS}}_F(a)} R_S^2 q_{S \cup T} &\le K_2. \label{eq:recursive-insertion-3}
    \end{align}
    For every $F \subseteq \mathcal{F}$ and $\varepsilon \in (0, 1)$, there is a sampler supported on $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Good}}(F)$ such that it outputs a sample whose law is within $\varepsilon$ in total variation distance of $\mu_F$ with expected running time $\ensuremath{\mathrm{poly}}\left(I, \varepsilon^{-1}, K_0, K_2, (1 - \kappa)^{-1}\right)$ with an absolute polynomial degree where $I$ is the length of the full input.
<!-- end theorem-4 -->
<!-- lemma-9: body_md -->
\label{lem:connected-set-identity}
    The following identity holds point-wisely on $\Omega$:
    \begin{align*}
        \ensuremath{\mathbf{1}}_{B_a} \cdot \ensuremath{\mathbf{1}}_{\ensuremath{\mathsf{Good}}(F \setminus \left\{a\right\})} = \sum_{S \in \ensuremath{\mathsf{CS}}_F(a)} (-1)^{\ab|S| - 1} \ensuremath{\mathbf{1}}_{E_S} \cdot \ensuremath{\mathbf{1}}_{\ensuremath{\mathsf{Good}}\left(F_S\right)}.
    \end{align*}
    Writing $F^{-} \triangleq F \setminus \left\{a\right\}, Z_{-} \triangleq Z_{F^{-}}$ and $r_{F^{-}, a} \triangleq \mu_{F^{-}}\left(B_a\right)$, we have
    \begin{align*}
        \frac{r_{F^{-}, a}}{1 - r_{F^{-}, a}} = \sum_{S \in \ensuremath{\mathsf{CS}}_F(a)} (-1)^{\ab|S| - 1} q_S R_S.
    \end{align*}
    Furthermore, if \eqref{eq:recursive-insertion-1} holds, then $Z_-/Z_F \le 1 + K_0$.
<!-- end lemma-9 -->
<!-- proof-10: body_md -->
To show the first identity, we follow the proof of \cite[Theorem 2.4]{JMP26} which comes from the inclusion-exclusion argument of \cite{LWYZZ26}.
    Fix an assignment $\sigma$.
    By the inclusion-exclusion principle,
    \begin{align*}
        \ensuremath{\mathbf{1}}_{B_a}(\sigma) \prod_{c \in F^{-}} \left(1 - \ensuremath{\mathbf{1}}_{B_c}(\sigma)\right) &= \sum_{S \subseteq F\; :\; a \in S} (-1)^{\ab|S| - 1} \prod_{c \in S} \ensuremath{\mathbf{1}}_{B_c}(\sigma) \\
        &= \sum_{S \subseteq \ensuremath{\mathsf{CS}}_F(a)} \sum_{T \subseteq F_S} (-1)^{\ab|S| + \ab|T| - 1} \prod_{c \in S} \ensuremath{\mathbf{1}}_{B_c}(\sigma) \prod_{c \in T} \ensuremath{\mathbf{1}}_{B_c}(\sigma) \\
        &= \sum_{S \subseteq \ensuremath{\mathsf{CS}}_F(a)} (-1)^{\ab|S| - 1} \ensuremath{\mathbf{1}}_{E_S}(\sigma) \cdot \ensuremath{\mathbf{1}}_{\ensuremath{\mathsf{Good}}\left(F_S\right)}(\sigma).
    \end{align*}

    To derive the second identity, note that $E_S$ depends only on variables used in constraints $S$.
    Then variables in $F_S = F \setminus N_G[S]$ are disjoint from those in $E_S$.
    Hence we obtain
    \begin{align*}
        \nu\left(E_S \cap \ensuremath{\mathsf{Good}}\left(F_S\right)\right) = \nu\left(E_S\right) \cdot \nu\left(\ensuremath{\mathsf{Good}}\left(F_S\right)\right) = q_S Z_{F_S}
    \end{align*}
    since $\nu$ is a product measure, $q_S = \nu\left(E_S\right)$ by \eqref{eq:insertion-factors} and $Z_{F_S} = \nu\left(\ensuremath{\mathsf{Good}}\left(F_S\right)\right)$.
    Taking expectation for the first identity, we have
    \begin{align*}
        Z_{F^{-}} - Z_F &= \sum_{S \in \ensuremath{\mathsf{CS}}_F(a)} (-1)^{\ab|S| - 1} \nu\left(E_S\right) \nu\left(\ensuremath{\mathsf{Good}}\left(F_S\right)\right) \\
        &= \sum_{S \in \ensuremath{\mathsf{CS}}_F(a)} (-1)^{\ab|S| - 1} q_S Z_{F_S}.
    \end{align*}
    Dividing $Z_F$ on two sides gives
    \begin{align*}
        \frac{r_{F^{-}, a}}{1 - r_{F^{-}, a}} = \sum_{S \in \ensuremath{\mathsf{CS}}_F(a)} (-1)^{\ab|S| - 1} q_S R_S
    \end{align*}
    by $R_S = Z_{F_S}/Z_F$ in \eqref{eq:insertion-factors}.

    For the ratio upper bound, since $F_S \subseteq F$ and thus $R_S \ge 1$, it holds that
    \begin{align*}
        \frac{Z_{-}}{Z_F} &= 1 + \sum_{S \in \ensuremath{\mathsf{CS}}_F(a)} (-1)^{\ab|S| - 1} q_S R_S \\
        &\le 1 + \sum_{S \in \ensuremath{\mathsf{CS}}_F(a)} (-1)^{\ab|S| - 1} q_S R_S^2 \\
        &\le 1 + K_0.
    \end{align*}
    Therefore, we conclude the lemma.
<!-- end proof-10 -->
<!-- lemma-10: body_md -->
\label{lem:parity-pairing}
    Suppose that $\sigma \in E_S \cap \ensuremath{\mathsf{Good}}\left(F_S\right)$.
    When $Q(\sigma) \neq \left\{a\right\}$, let $d_\sigma(S)$ be the last member of $Q(\sigma) \setminus \left\{a\right\}$ explored by $S$, and define $\iota_\sigma(S) \triangleq S \triangle \left\{d_\sigma(S)\right\}$.
    Then $\iota_\sigma(S) \in \ensuremath{\mathsf{CS}}_F(a)$, $\sigma \in E_{\iota_\sigma(S)} \cap \ensuremath{\mathsf{Good}}\left(F_{\iota_\sigma(S)}\right)$ and $\iota_\sigma\left(\iota_\sigma(S)\right) = S$.
    Trivially $S$ and $\iota_\sigma(S)$ have opposite parity.

    When $Q(\sigma) = \left\{a\right\}$, the only possible choice of $S$ is the singleton set $\left\{a\right\}$.
<!-- end lemma-10 -->
<!-- proof-11: body_md -->
The statement $\sigma \in E_S \cap \ensuremath{\mathsf{Good}}\left(F_S\right)$ is equivalent to
    \begin{align*}
        S \subseteq Q(\sigma) \subseteq N_F[S].
    \end{align*}
    Therefore all constraints in $Q(\sigma)$ are labeled in the exploration by $S$.
    When $Q(\sigma) \neq \left\{a\right\}$, write $S' = S \triangle \left\{d_\sigma(S)\right\}$.
    We verify the following properties of $S'$.
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item $S' \in \ensuremath{\mathsf{CS}}_F(a)$.
        Let $A$ be the accepted set immediately before $d_\sigma(S)$ is explored.
        Note that there is no accepted vertex after $d_\sigma(S)$ is explored (since $S \subseteq Q(\sigma)$).
        Therefore, it must be $S' = A$ or $S' = A \cup \left\{d_\sigma(S)\right\}$ and $d_\sigma(S)$ must be adjacent to $A$.
        It is clear that $a \in A$ by the definition of $d_\sigma(S)$.
        Hence we conclude that $a \in S'$ and $S'$ is connected, leading to the property $S' \in \ensuremath{\mathsf{CS}}_F(a)$.

        \item $\sigma \in E_{S'} \cap \ensuremath{\mathsf{Good}}\left(F_{S'}\right)$.
        It is equivalent to show that
        \begin{align*}
            S' \subseteq Q(\sigma) \subseteq N_F[S'].
        \end{align*}
        Since $A \subseteq S \subseteq Q(\sigma)$ and $d_\sigma(S) \subseteq Q(\sigma)$, we directly obtain that $S' \subseteq Q(\sigma)$.
        To prove $Q(\sigma) \subseteq N_F[S']$, recall the exploration of $S$ and the definition that $d_\sigma(S)$ is the last member of $Q(\sigma) \setminus \left\{a\right\}$ in the exploration.
        Then all clauses in $Q(\sigma) \setminus \left\{a\right\}$ were explored and thus labeled by the time $d_\sigma(S)$ was explored.
        Hence $Q \subseteq N_F[A] \subseteq N_F[S']$ by $A \subseteq S'$.

        \item $\iota_\sigma\left(\iota_\sigma(S)\right) = S$.
        Compare the explorations of $S$ and $S'$.
        Their membership decisions differ only at $d_\sigma(S)$.
        Then until $d_\sigma(S)$ is explored, they share the same accepted set and label set.
        Moreover, under the fixed total order on $F$, their exploration sequences keep identical through the selection of $d_\sigma(S)$.

        At $d_\sigma(S)$, the accept/reject decision is reversed.
        Since either $S' = A$ or $S' = A \cup \left\{d_\sigma(S)\right\}$ holds, any subsequent candidate is rejected.
        Therefore, we obtain that $d_{\sigma}\left(S'\right) = d_\sigma(S)$.
        Hence we conclude the property that $\iota_\sigma\left(\iota_\sigma(S)\right) = S$.
    \end{enumerate}

    When $Q(\sigma) = \left\{a\right\}$, there must hold $S \subseteq \left\{a\right\} \subseteq N_F[S]$, meaning that $S$ must be $\left\{a\right\}$.
<!-- end proof-11 -->
<!-- lemma-11: body_md -->
\label{lem:lists-enumeration}
    For every corridor label $e = (S, u)$, it holds that
    \begin{align*}
         
    \ensuremath{\mathbb{P}\ifblank{e \in \mathbf{L}}{}{_{e \in \mathbf{L}}}\left(#2\right)} 
 = q_e, \quad \ensuremath{\mathsf{Law}}\ab(\mathbf{L} \mid e \in \mathbf{L}) = \ensuremath{\mathsf{Law}}\left(\mathbf{L} \;\text{with}\; \sigma\left(\mathcal{V}_S\right) = u\;\text{fixed}\right).
    \end{align*}
    A realized random list $\mathbf{L}$ is enumerable in polynomial time of $\ab|\mathbf{L}|$ and the input length $I$.
    Moreover, if \eqref{eq:recursive-insertion-3} holds,
    \begin{align*}
         
    \ensuremath{\mathbb{E}\ifblank{\ab|\mathbf{L}|^2}{}{_{\ab|\mathbf{L}|^2}}\left[#2\right]} 
 \le K_2, \quad \sum_{e} q_e  
    \ensuremath{\mathbb{E}\ifblank{1 + \ab|\mathbf{L}| \mid e \in \mathbf{L}}{}{_{1 + \ab|\mathbf{L}| \mid e \in \mathbf{L}}}\left[#2\right]} 
 \le K_2 + \sqrt{K_2}.
    \end{align*}
<!-- end lemma-11 -->
<!-- proof-12: body_md -->
The argument $ 
    \ensuremath{\mathbb{P}\ifblank{e \in \mathbf{L}}{}{_{e \in \mathbf{L}}}\left(#2\right)} 
 = q_e$ holds trivially by the definition of $q_e$.
    Since $u \in E_S$, the event $\left\{u \in \mathbf{L}\right\}$ is exactly the event $\left\{\sigma\left(\mathcal{V}_S\right) = u\right\}$.
    By the product independence of $\nu$, for every possible list $L$,
    \begin{align*}
         
    \ensuremath{\mathbb{P}\ifblank{\mathbf{L} = L \mid e \in \mathbf{L}}{}{_{\mathbf{L} = L \mid e \in \mathbf{L}}}\left(#2\right)} 
 =  
    \ensuremath{\mathbb{P}\ifblank{\mathbf{L} = L \land \sigma\left(\mathcal{V}_S\right) = u}{}{_{\mathbf{L} = L \land \sigma\left(\mathcal{V}_S\right) = u}}\left(#2\right)} 
.
    \end{align*}
    Thus we conclude the second argument for the law of random lists.

    To enumerate the entries in $\mathbf{L}$, firstly test all constraints under the assignment $\sigma$ and form the violating set $Q(\sigma)$ in time $\ensuremath{\mathrm{poly}}\left(I\right)$ where $I$ is the input length.
    If $a \notin Q(\sigma)$, $\mathbf{L}$ is the empty list.
    Otherwise,
    \begin{align*}
        \mathbf{L} = \left\{\left(S, \sigma\left(\mathcal{V}_S\right)\right) \mid S \in \ensuremath{\mathsf{CS}}_F(a), \sigma \in E_S\right\}.
    \end{align*}
    It suffices to enumerate every connected subset containing $a$ in $\mathcal{H}[Q(\sigma)]$.
    Using a depth-first search, it takes at most $2\ab|\mathbf{L}|$ time to enumerate all entries in $\mathbf{L}$ and therefore $\mathbf{L}$ is enumerable in $\ensuremath{\mathrm{poly}}\left(I\right)\left(1 + \ab|\mathbf{L}|\right)$.

    When \eqref{eq:recursive-insertion-3} holds, by the fact that $R_S \ge 1$,
    \begin{align*}
         
    \ensuremath{\mathbb{E}\ifblank{\ab|\mathbf{L}|^2}{}{_{\ab|\mathbf{L}|^2}}\left[#2\right]} 
 = \sum_{S, T \in \ensuremath{\mathsf{CS}}_F(a)} q_{S \cup T} \le \sum_{S, T \in \ensuremath{\mathsf{CS}}_F(a)} R_S^2 q_{S \cup T} \le K_2.
    \end{align*}
    For the last inequality, by law of total expectation,
    \begin{align*}
        \sum_{e} q_e  
    \ensuremath{\mathbb{E}\ifblank{1 + \ab|\mathbf{L}| \mid e \in \mathbf{L}}{}{_{1 + \ab|\mathbf{L}| \mid e \in \mathbf{L}}}\left[#2\right]} 
 &=  
    \ensuremath{\mathbb{E}\ifblank{\ab|\mathbf{L}| + \ab|\mathbf{L}|^2}{}{_{\ab|\mathbf{L}| + \ab|\mathbf{L}|^2}}\left[#2\right]} 
 \\
        &\le  
    \ensuremath{\mathbb{E}\ifblank{\ab|\mathbf{L}|^2}{}{_{\ab|\mathbf{L}|^2}}\left[#2\right]} 
 + \sqrt{ 
    \ensuremath{\mathbb{E}\ifblank{\ab|\mathbf{L}|^2}{}{_{\ab|\mathbf{L}|^2}}\left[#2\right]} 
} \\
        &\le K_2 + \sqrt{K_2} 
    \end{align*}
    using $ 
    \ensuremath{\mathbb{E}\ifblank{\ab|\mathbf{L}|}{}{_{\ab|\mathbf{L}|}}\left[#2\right]} 
 \le \sqrt{ 
    \ensuremath{\mathbb{E}\ifblank{\ab|\mathbf{L}|^2}{}{_{\ab|\mathbf{L}|^2}}\left[#2\right]} 
}$.
<!-- end proof-12 -->
<!-- definition-1: body_md -->
\label{def:state-space}
    We define the state space $\mathscr{S}(F, a)$ inductively on $\ab|F|$.
    \begin{itemize}
        \item \textbf{Input and output states:}
        \begin{align*}
            \forall \sigma \in \ensuremath{\mathsf{Good}}\left(F^-\right),\; \mathsf{I}(\sigma), \quad \text{and} \quad \forall \sigma \in \ensuremath{\mathsf{Good}}(F),\; \mathsf{O}(\sigma).
        \end{align*}
        Furthermore, define $\mu_-\left(\mathsf{I}(\sigma)\right) = \mu_{F^{-}}(\sigma)$ on the input space and $\mu_+\left(\mathsf{O}(\sigma)\right) = \mu_F(\sigma)$ on the output space.

        \item \textbf{Set states:} for every connected subset $S \in \ensuremath{\mathsf{CS}}_F(a)$, there are states $\mathsf{T}_S(\sigma)$ for $\sigma \in E_S \cap \ensuremath{\mathsf{Good}}\left(F_S\right)$.
        
        \item \textbf{Layers:} order $N_F[S] \setminus \left\{a\right\}$ as $b_{S, 1}, \ldots, b_{S, \ell(S)}$.
        Set
        \begin{align*}
            \forall i = 0, \ldots, \ell(S), \quad F_{S, i} = F_S \cup \left\{b_{S, 1}, \ldots, b_{S, i}\right\}.
        \end{align*}
        For each label $e = (S, u)$ we introduce layers
        \begin{align*}
            \forall i = 0, \ldots, \ell(S), \sigma \in \ensuremath{\mathsf{Good}}\left(F_{S, i}\right), \quad \mathsf{L}_i^e(\sigma).
        \end{align*}
        For every $i = 1, \ldots, \ell(S)$, we embed a distinct copy of $\mathscr{S}\left(F_{S, i}, b_{S, i}\right)$ by a map $\psi_{e, i}$ that identifies its input $\mathsf{I}(\sigma)$ with $\mathsf{L}_{i - 1}^e(\sigma)$ and its output $\mathsf{O}(\sigma)$ with $\mathsf{L}_i^e(\sigma)$.
        Every other state $X$ becomes a distinct state $(e, i, X)$.
    \end{itemize}
<!-- end definition-1 -->
<!-- definition-2: body_md -->
\label{def:transition-rule}
    Consider the state space $\mathscr{S}(F, i)$.
    There are three kinds of transition rules $\mathsf{F}, \mathsf{B}$ and $\mathsf{R}$ (forward, backward and reset):
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item At $\mathsf{I}(\sigma)$, Rule $\mathsf{F}$ moves to $\mathsf{O}(\sigma)$ if $\sigma \notin B_a$ and otherwise to $\mathsf{T}_{\left\{a\right\}}(\sigma)$.
        Rule $\mathsf{R}$ generates a fresh list $\mathbf{L}$ and if it is non-empty, choose $e$ uniformly in $\mathbf{L}$ and move to $\mathsf{L}_{\ell(S)}^e(\sigma)$.

        \item At $\mathsf{O}(\sigma)$, Rule $\mathsf{B}$ moves to $\mathsf{I}(\sigma)$.

        \item At $\mathsf{T}_S(\sigma)$, write $\sigma = (y, z)$ where $y$ is on $\mathcal{V} \setminus \mathcal{V}_S$ and $z$ on $\mathcal{V}_S$.
        Rule $\mathsf{F}$ samples $u \sim \nu_{\mathcal{V}_S}$ and moves to $\mathsf{L}_0^{(S, z)}(y, u)$.
        Rule $\mathsf{B}$ moves to $\mathsf{I}(\sigma)$ if $Q(\sigma) = \left\{a\right\}$ and to $\mathsf{T}_{\iota_\sigma(S)}(\sigma)$ otherwise.

        \item At $\mathsf{L}_i^e(\sigma)$ where $e = (S, u)$:
        \begin{enumerate}
            \item Rule $\mathsf{B}$ moves to $\mathsf{L}_{i - 1}^e(\sigma)$ if $i > 0$.
            If $i = 0$, write $\sigma = (y, z)$ and restore the saved value, moving to $\mathsf{T}_S(y, u)$.

            \item If $i < \ell(S)$ and the rule is $\mathsf{F}$ or $\mathsf{R}$, apply the same rule to the input states $\mathsf{I}(\sigma)$ of $\mathscr{S}\left(F_{S, i + 1}, b_{S, i + 1}\right)$ and embed the result by $\psi_{e, i + 1}$.

            \item If $i = \ell(S)$ and the rule is $\mathsf{F}$, generate $\mathbf{L}^e \sim \ensuremath{\mathsf{Law}}(\mathbf{L} \;|\; e \in \mathbf{L})$.
            Pick a uniform label in $\mathbf{L}^e$ and move to $\mathsf{I}(\sigma)$ if and only if the chosen label is $e$.
        \end{enumerate}

        \item At an internal state $(e, i, X)$, apply the rule to $X$ in its smaller copy and embed the result by $\psi_{e, i}$.
    \end{enumerate}
<!-- end definition-2 -->
<!-- lemma-12: body_md -->
\label{lem:detailed-balance}
    The kernel $P_{F, a}$ is reversible with respect to the weight $w$ and $K_\beta$ is reversible with respect to $\pi = \widetilde{w}/M$.
    Furthermore, conditional on outer output states, $\pi$ gives the distribution $\mu_+$.
<!-- end lemma-12 -->
<!-- proof-13: body_md -->
We adapt the proof of \cite[Lemma 6.4]{JMP26} to our setting by verifying the detailed balance equation for $P_{F, a}$ with respect to $w$:
    \begin{align} \label{eq:detailed-balanced-equation}
        \forall X, Y \in \mathscr{S}(F, a), \quad w(X) P_{F, a}(X, Y) = w(Y) P_{F, a}(Y, X)
    \end{align}
    by induction on the cardinality of $F$.
    \begin{itemize}
        \item \textbf{Direct, singleton and pairing transitions.}
        There are three kinds of transitions:
        \begin{align*}
            \mathsf{I}(\sigma) &\longleftrightarrow \mathsf{O}(\sigma), \\
            \mathsf{I}(\sigma) &\longleftrightarrow \mathsf{T}_{\left\{a\right\}}(\sigma), \\
            \mathsf{T}_S(\sigma) &\longleftrightarrow \mathsf{T}_{\iota(S)}(\sigma).
        \end{align*}
        In fact, by \Cref{def:transition-rule}, when the transition $\mathsf{I}(\sigma) \to \mathsf{O}(\sigma)$ is legal, $\sigma$ satisfies all of $F$ and thus $\mathsf{O}(\sigma)$ can move to $\mathsf{I}(\sigma)$ as well.
        Otherwise, the second transition occurs under the rule $\mathsf{F}$.
        Under the rule $\mathsf{B}$, $\mathsf{T}_{\left\{a\right\}}(\sigma)$ can move to $\mathsf{I}(\sigma)$.

        The third transition occurs under the rule $\mathsf{B}$ and by \Cref{lem:parity-pairing}, the reverse transition occurs.

        By direct calculation, \eqref{eq:detailed-balanced-equation} holds for all of three kinds of transitions.

        \item \textbf{Resampling transitions.}
        Fix a corridor label $e = (S, u)$ and write the current assignment as $\sigma = (y, u)$ where $y$ is on $\mathcal{V} \setminus \mathcal{V}_S$ and $u$ is on $\mathcal{V}_S$.
        The forward rule $\mathsf{F}$ resamples the coordinates in $\mathcal{V}_S$, produces $z \sim \nu_{\mathcal{V}_S}$ and forms the transition
        \begin{align*}
            \mathsf{T}_S(y, u) \to \mathsf{L}_0^e(y, z)
        \end{align*}
        with transition probability
        \begin{align*}
            P_{F, a}\ab(\mathsf{T}_S(y, u), \mathsf{L}_0^e(y, z)) = \frac{\nu_{\mathcal{V}_S}(z)}{6}.
        \end{align*}
        The reverse transition restores the saved value $u$, so
        \begin{align*}
            P_{F, a}\ab(\mathsf{L}_0^e(y, z), \mathsf{T}_S(y, u)) = \frac{1}{6}.
        \end{align*}
        Their corresponding weights are $w\left(\mathsf{T}_S(y, u)\right) = \nu(y, u)$ and $w\left(\mathsf{L}_0^e(y, z)\right) = q_e \nu(y, z)$.
        Since $\nu$ is a product measure and $q_e = \nu_{\mathcal{V}_S}(u)$, it holds that
        \begin{align*}
            \nu(y, u) \nu_{\mathcal{V}_S}(z) = \nu_{\mathcal{V} \setminus \mathcal{V}_S}(y) \nu_{\mathcal{V}_S}(u) \nu_{\mathcal{V}_S}(z) = q_e \nu(y, z).
        \end{align*}
        Therefore \eqref{eq:detailed-balanced-equation} holds for all resampling transitions.

        \item \textbf{Random-list reset and return transitions.}
        Fix a corridor label $e = (S, u)$ and consider
        \begin{align*}
            \mathsf{I}(\sigma) \longleftrightarrow \mathsf{L}_{\ell(S)}^e(\sigma).
        \end{align*}
        Recall the definition of $\omega_e$.
        The reset rule $\mathsf{R}$ applied to the state $\mathsf{I}(\sigma)$ gives that
        \begin{align*}
            P_{F, a}\ab(\mathsf{I}(\sigma), \mathsf{L}_{\ell(S)}^e(\sigma)) = \frac{\omega_e}{6}.
        \end{align*}
        For the reverse direction, the forward rule $\mathsf{F}$ generates a fresh list $\mathbf{L}^e$ and choose a uniform entry.
        When the chosen entry is exactly $e$, $\mathsf{F}$ makes a move from $\mathsf{L}_{\ell(S)}^e(\sigma)$ to $\mathsf{I}(\sigma)$.
        Then the transition probability is
        \begin{align*}
            P_{F, a}\ab(\mathsf{L}_{\ell(S)}^e(\sigma), \mathsf{I}(\sigma)) = \frac{1}{6} \cdot  
    \ensuremath{\mathbb{E}\ifblank{\ab|\mathbf{L}|^{-1} \mid e \in \mathbf{L}}{}{_{\ab|\mathbf{L}|^{-1} \mid e \in \mathbf{L}}}\left[#2\right]} 
 = \frac{ 
    \ensuremath{\mathbb{E}\ifblank{\mathbbm{1}\left[e \in \mathbf{L}\right] \cdot \ab|\mathbf{L}|^{-1}}{}{_{\mathbbm{1}\left[e \in \mathbf{L}\right] \cdot \ab|\mathbf{L}|^{-1}}}\left[#2\right]} 
}{ 
    \ensuremath{\mathbb{P}\ifblank{e \in \mathbf{L}}{}{_{e \in \mathbf{L}}}\left(#2\right)} 
} = \frac{q_e}{\omega_e}.
        \end{align*}
        Using weights $w\left(\mathsf{I}(\sigma)\right) = \nu(\sigma)$ and $w\left(\mathsf{L}_{\ell(S)}^e(\sigma)\right) = q_e \nu(\sigma)$, \eqref{eq:detailed-balanced-equation} holds for all random-list reset and return transitions.

        \item \textbf{Transitions inside smaller copies.}
        By induction, since a smaller copy $\mathscr{S}\left(F_{S, i}, b_{S, i}\right)$ has a smaller cardinality $\ab|F_{S, i}| \le \ab|F| - 1 < \ab|F|$, it is easy to verify that all detail balanced equations still hold under the mapping $\psi_{e, i}$.
    \end{itemize}
    Therefore, we conclude that $P_{F, a}$ is reversible with respect to $w$.
    The reversibility of $K_\beta$ with respect to $\pi$ can be verified in a similar way and for simplicity of the proof, we omit here.

    Conditional on the outer output states, for every assignment $\sigma \in \ensuremath{\mathsf{Good}}(F)$,
    \begin{align*}
        \pi\left(\mathsf{O}(\sigma)\right) = \frac{\nu(\sigma)}{\beta M}
    \end{align*}
    and therefore $\sum_{\tau \in \ensuremath{\mathsf{Good}}(F)} \pi\left(\mathsf{O}(\tau)\right) = Z_F/(\beta M)$.
    Hence
    \begin{align*}
        \pi\ab(\mathsf{O}(\sigma) \mid \text{the outer output states}) = \frac{\pi\left(\mathsf{O}(\sigma)\right)}{\sum_{\tau \in \ensuremath{\mathsf{Good}}(F)} \pi\left(\mathsf{O}(\tau)\right)} = \frac{\nu(\sigma)}{Z_F} = \mu_F(\sigma)
    \end{align*}
    which is exactly $\mu_+$.
<!-- end proof-13 -->
<!-- lemma-13: body_md -->
\label{lem:small-outside-weight}
    Assume that \eqref{eq:recursive-insertion-1} and \eqref{eq:recursive-insertion-2} hold.
    Then it holds that
    \begin{align*}
        \sum_{X \in \mathscr{S}(F, a) \setminus \left\{\mathsf{O}(\sigma) \;:\; \sigma \in \ensuremath{\mathsf{Good}}(F)\right\}} w(X) \le \frac{1 + 2K_0}{1 - \kappa} \cdot Z_-.
    \end{align*}
    Consequently $M \le \left(\beta^{-1} + \left(1 + 2K_0\right)/(1 - \kappa)\right) Z_-$ and therefore
    \begin{align*}
        \ab\|\pi - \mu_+\|_{\mathrm{TV}} \le \frac{\beta\left(1 + 2K_0\right)\left(1 + K_0\right)}{1 - \kappa}.
    \end{align*}
<!-- end lemma-13 -->
<!-- proof-14: body_md -->
Fix $F \subseteq \mathcal{F}$ and $a \in F$.
    We show by induction on $\ab|F|$,
    \begin{align*}
        \sum_{X \in \mathscr{S}(F, a) \setminus \left\{\mathsf{O}(\sigma) \;:\; \sigma \in \ensuremath{\mathsf{Good}}(F)\right\}} w(X) \le \frac{1 + 2K_0}{1 - \kappa} \cdot Z_-.
    \end{align*}
    By \Cref{def:state-space}, the counted sets can be partitioned into the following groups:
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item The input states $\mathsf{I}$: by definition, the total weight of input states is
        \begin{align*}
            \sum_{\sigma \in \ensuremath{\mathsf{Good}}\left(F^-\right)} w\left(\mathsf{I}(\sigma)\right) = \sum_{\sigma \in \ensuremath{\mathsf{Good}}\left(F^-\right)} \nu(\sigma) = Z_-.
        \end{align*}

        \item The set states $\mathsf{T}_S$ for every $S \in \ensuremath{\mathsf{CS}}_F(a)$: for a fixed connected subset $S \in \ensuremath{\mathsf{CS}}_F(a)$, the set states have assignments $\sigma \in E_S \cap \ensuremath{\mathsf{Good}}\left(F_S\right)$ with weight $\nu(\sigma)$.
        The total weight of $\mathsf{T}_S$ is
        \begin{align*}
            \sum_{\sigma \in E_S \cap \ensuremath{\mathsf{Good}}\left(F_S\right)} \nu(\sigma) = \nu\left(E_S \cap \ensuremath{\mathsf{Good}}\left(F_S\right)\right) = q_S Z_{F_S}
        \end{align*}
        since $\nu$ is a product measure.
        
        \item The last layer states $\mathsf{L}_{\ell(S)}^e$ for every corridor label $e$: fix a label $e = (S, u)$.
        Observe that $F_{S, \ell(S)} = F \setminus \left\{a\right\} = F^-$.
        Then its layer has total weight
        \begin{align*}
            \sum_{\sigma \in \ensuremath{\mathsf{Good}}\left(F^-\right)} w\ab(\mathsf{L}_{\ell(S)}^e(\sigma)) = \sum_{\sigma \in \ensuremath{\mathsf{Good}}\left(F^-\right)} q_e \nu(\sigma) = q_e Z_-.
        \end{align*}
        Summing over all possible corridor labels,
        \begin{align*}
            \sum_{e = (S, u)} q_e Z_- = Z_- \sum_{u \in E_S} \nu_{\mathcal{V}_S}(u) = q_S Z_-.
        \end{align*}

        \item The states of each smaller embed copy excluding that copy's output states: copy $(e, i)$ is a scaled copy of the insertion construction for $\left(F_{S, i}, b_{S, i}\right)$.
        By induction, its weight outside its own output is at most $\left(1 + 2K_0\right) Z_{F_{S, i - 1}}/(1 - \kappa)$.
        Therefore, this copy contributes to the original states at most
        \begin{align*}
            q_e \frac{\left(1 + 2K_0\right)}{1 - \kappa} \cdot Z_{F_{S, i - 1}}.
        \end{align*}
    \end{enumerate}

    Put all of four cases together, and we obtain that
    \begin{align*}
        \sum_{X \in \mathscr{S}(F, a) \setminus \left\{\mathsf{O}(\sigma) \;:\; \sigma \in \ensuremath{\mathsf{Good}}(F)\right\}} w(X) &\le Z_- + \sum_{S \in \ensuremath{\mathsf{CS}}_F(a)} \ab(q_S Z_{F_S} + q_S Z_- + \sum_{e = (S, u)} \sum_{i = 1}^{\ell(S)} q_e \frac{1 + 2K_0}{1 - \kappa} Z_{F_{S, i - 1}}) \\
        &\le Z_- + \sum_{S \in \ensuremath{\mathsf{CS}}_F(a)} q_S Z_{F_S}\ab(2 + \frac{1 + 2K_0}{1 - \kappa} \ell(S)).
    \end{align*}
    The last inequality holds since $F_S \subseteq F_{S, i - 1} \subseteq F_-$ and therefore $Z_- \le Z_{F_{S, i - 1}} \le Z_{F_S}$.
    By the fact $Z_F \le Z_-$ and $R_S \ge 1$,
    \begin{align*}
        \frac{q_S Z_{F_S}}{Z_-} \le \frac{q_S Z_{F_S}}{Z_F} = q_S R_S \le q_S R_S^2.
    \end{align*}
    Therefore, by \eqref{eq:recursive-insertion-1} and \eqref{eq:recursive-insertion-2},
    \begin{align*}
        \sum_{X \in \mathscr{S}(F, a) \setminus \left\{\mathsf{O}(\sigma) \;:\; \sigma \in \ensuremath{\mathsf{Good}}(F)\right\}} w(X) &\le Z_-\ab(1 + \sum_{S \in \ensuremath{\mathsf{CS}}_F(a)} q_S R_S^2\ab(2 + \frac{1 + 2K_0}{1 - \kappa} \ell(S))) \\
        &\le Z_- \ab(1 + 2K_0 + \kappa \cdot \frac{1 + 2K_0}{1 - \kappa}) \\
        &= \frac{1 + 2K_0}{1 - \kappa} Z_-.
    \end{align*}
    
    To show the upper bound of $M$, we observe
    \begin{align*}
        \sum_{\sigma \in \ensuremath{\mathsf{Good}}(F)} \widetilde{w}\left(\mathsf{O}(\sigma)\right) = \sum_{\sigma \in \ensuremath{\mathsf{Good}}(F)} \frac{\nu(\sigma)}{\beta} = \beta^{-1} Z_F.
    \end{align*}
    Therefore, by the fact $Z_F \le Z_-$,
    \begin{align*}
        M \le \frac{1 + 2K_0}{1 - \kappa} Z_- + \beta^{-1} Z_F \le \ab(\beta^{-1} + \frac{1 + 2K_0}{1 - \kappa}) Z_-.
    \end{align*}
    By \Cref{lem:detailed-balance}, a coupling argument provides that the total variation distance is at most the probability of being outside the outputs.
    Hence, by the inequality $Z_-/Z_F \le 1 + K_0$ in \Cref{lem:connected-set-identity},
    \begin{align*}
        \ab\|\pi - \mu_+\|_{\mathrm{TV}} \le \frac{\beta \left(1 + 2K_0\right)/(1 - \kappa) Z_-}{Z_F} \le \frac{\beta\left(1 + 2K_0\right)\left(1 + K_0\right)}{1 - \kappa}.
    \end{align*}
    Thus we conclude the lemma.
<!-- end proof-14 -->
<!-- lemma-14: body_md -->
\label{lem:flow-bound}
    Assume that \eqref{eq:recursive-insertion-1}, \eqref{eq:recursive-insertion-2} and \eqref{eq:recursive-insertion-3} hold.
    There is a signed flow $\varphi$ satisfying that $\partial{\varphi} = \mu_- - \mu_+$ and its energy is at most
    \begin{align*}
        \ensuremath{\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{flow}}}(\varphi) \le \frac{6 + 18K_0 + 6K_2}{(1 - \kappa) Z_-}.
    \end{align*}
<!-- end lemma-14 -->
<!-- proof-15: body_md -->
We employ the induction on $\ab|F|$.
    For a corridor label $e = (S, u)$, put $\gamma_e \triangleq (-1)^{\ab|S| - 1} R_S q_e$.
    Assign the following values:
    \begin{align*}
        \varphi\left(\mathsf{I}(\sigma), \mathsf{O}(\sigma)\right) &= \frac{\nu(\sigma)}{Z_F}, \\
        \varphi\left(\mathsf{I}(\sigma), \mathsf{T}_{\left\{a\right\}}(\sigma)\right) &= \frac{\nu(\sigma)}{Z_F}, \\
        \varphi\left(\mathsf{T}_S(\sigma), \mathsf{T}_{\iota(S)}(\sigma)\right) &= (-1)^{\ab|S|}\frac{\nu(\sigma)}{Z_F}, \\
        \varphi\left(\mathsf{T}_S(y, u), \mathsf{L}_0^e(y, z)\right) &= \gamma_e \mu_{F_S}(y, z), \\
        \varphi\left(\mathsf{L}_{\ell(S)}^e(\sigma), \mathsf{I}(\sigma)\right) &= \gamma_e \mu_-(\sigma), \\
        \varphi\left(\psi_{e, i}(X), \psi_{e, i}(Y)\right) &= \gamma_e \varphi_{F_{S, i}, b_{S, i}}(X, Y)
    \end{align*}
    where $\varphi_{F_{S, i}, b_{S, i}}$ is the flow from the induction.

    The divergence identity holds by a similar argument as \cite[Lemma 6.6]{JMP26}.
    For simplicity, we omit here.
    To bound the energy, for the direct input-output transitions, their total energy contributes to $\ensuremath{\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{flow}}}(\varphi)$ at most
    \begin{align*}
        \sum_{\sigma \in \ensuremath{\mathsf{Good}}(F)} \frac{\left(\nu(\sigma)/Z_F\right)^2}{\nu(\sigma)/6} = \frac{6}{Z_F}.
    \end{align*}
    A singleton or pairing edge incident to $\mathsf{T}_S(\sigma)$ also has capacity $\nu(\sigma)/6$ and therefore energy $6\nu(\sigma)/Z_F^2$.
    Summing over all set states, they contributes at most
    \begin{align*}
        \sum_{S \in \ensuremath{\mathsf{CS}}_F(a)} \frac{6 q_S Z_{F_S}}{Z_F^2} = 6 \sum_{S \in \ensuremath{\mathsf{CS}}_F(a)} \frac{q_S R_S^2}{Z_{F_S}}.
    \end{align*}
    For a resampling edge, the capacity is $q_e \nu(y, z)/6$.
    Then for a fixed corridor label $e = (S, u)$, the total contribution of $e$ is 
    \begin{align*}
        \sum_{(y, z) \in \ensuremath{\mathsf{Good}}\left(F_S\right)} \frac{\gamma_e^2 \left(\nu\left(y, z\right)\right)^2 / Z_{F_S}^2}{q_e \nu(y, z)/6} = \frac{6 q_e R_S^2}{Z_{F_S}}
    \end{align*}
    The total contribution to $\ensuremath{\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{flow}}}(\varphi)$ of all three kinds of edges is at most
    \begin{align*}
        \frac{6}{Z_F} + 6\sum_{S \in \ensuremath{\mathsf{CS}}_F(a)} \frac{q_S R_S^2}{Z_{F_S}} + 6\sum_{S \in \ensuremath{\mathsf{CS}}_F(a)} \frac{q_S R_S^2}{Z_{F_S}} \le \frac{6 + 18K_0}{Z_-}
    \end{align*}
    using $Z_{F_S} \ge Z_-$, $\sum_{S} q_S R_S^2 \le K_0$ and $Z_-/Z_F \le 1 + K_0$.

    For return edges, its capacity is $\omega_e \nu(\sigma)/6$.
    Then summing over all $\sigma$ gives that the total contribution is
    \begin{align*}
        \sum_{\sigma \in \ensuremath{\mathsf{Good}}\left(F^-\right)} \frac{q_e^2 R_S^2 \left(\nu\left(\sigma\right)\right)^2 / Z_-^2}{\omega_e \nu(\sigma)/6} = \frac{6 q_e^2 R_S^2}{\omega_e Z_-}.
    \end{align*}
    By the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality,
    \begin{align*}
        q_e^2 &= \ab( 
    \ensuremath{\mathbb{E}\ifblank{\frac{\mathbbm{1}\left[e \in \mathbf{L}\right]}{\sqrt{\ab|\mathbf{L}|}} \sqrt{\ab|\mathbf{L}|}}{}{_{\frac{\mathbbm{1}\left[e \in \mathbf{L}\right]}{\sqrt{\ab|\mathbf{L}|}} \sqrt{\ab|\mathbf{L}|}}}\left[#2\right]} 
)^2 \\
        &\le \omega_e  
    \ensuremath{\mathbb{E}\ifblank{\mathbbm{1}\left[e \in \mathbf{L}\right] \cdot \ab|\mathbf{L}|}{}{_{\mathbbm{1}\left[e \in \mathbf{L}\right] \cdot \ab|\mathbf{L}|}}\left[#2\right]} 
.
    \end{align*}
    Summing over all retained corridor labels,
    \begin{align*}
        \sum_{e = (S, u)} \frac{q_e^2 R_S^2}{\omega_e} &\le  
    \ensuremath{\mathbb{E}\ifblank{\ab|\mathbf{L}| \cdot \sum_{(S, u) \in \mathbf{L}} R_S^2}{}{_{\ab|\mathbf{L}| \cdot \sum_{(S, u) \in \mathbf{L}} R_S^2}}\left[#2\right]} 
 \\
        &= \sum_{S, T \in \ensuremath{\mathsf{CS}}_F(a)} q_{S \cup T} R_S^2 \\
        &\le K_2.
    \end{align*}

    Finally, to bound the recursive smaller copies, fix a copy $(e, i)$.
    By induction and multiplication factors from $\psi_{e, i}$, the total energy is at most
    \begin{align*}
        R_S^2 q_e \cdot \frac{6 + 18K_0 + 6K_2}{(1 - \kappa)Z_{F_{S, i - 1}}}
    \end{align*}
    Since $Z_{F_{S, i - 1}} \ge Z_-$, summing over all labels and positions,
    \begin{align*}
        \sum_{e = (S, u)} \sum_{i = 1}^{\ell(S)} R_S^2 q_e \cdot \frac{6 + 18K_0 + 6K_2}{(1 - \kappa)Z_{F_{S, i - 1}}} &\le \frac{6 + 18K_0 + 6K_2}{(1 - \kappa)Z_-} \sum_{S \in \ensuremath{\mathsf{CS}}_F(a)} q_S R_S^2 \ell(S) \\
        &\le \kappa \cdot \frac{6 + 18K_0 + 6K_2}{(1 - \kappa)Z_-}.
    \end{align*}

    Putting all together,
    \begin{align*}
        Z_- \ensuremath{\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{flow}}}(\varphi) \le 6 + 18K_0 + 6K_2 + \kappa \cdot \frac{6 + 18K_0 + 6K_2}{1 - \kappa} = \frac{6 + 18K_0 + 6K_2}{1 - \kappa}.
    \end{align*}
    Thus we conclude the lemma.
<!-- end proof-15 -->
<!-- proposition-3: body_md -->
\label{prop:flow-convergence}
    Let $K$ be a finite lazy reversible kernel with positive stationary distribution $\pi$.
    Suppose that a signed flow $\varphi$ has energy at most $A$ with capacities $\pi(X) K(X, Y)$.
    Then
    \begin{align*}
        \forall N \in \mathbb{N}, \quad \ab\|(\partial{\varphi}) K^N\|_{\mathrm{TV}} \le \frac{1}{2} \sqrt{\frac{A}{2N + 1}}.
    \end{align*}
    In particular, if $\partial{\varphi} = \mu_- - \mu_+$,
    \begin{align*}
        \ab\|\mu_- K^N - \mu_+\|_{\mathrm{TV}} \le \frac{1}{2} \sqrt{\frac{A}{2N + 1}} + 2\ab\|\pi - \mu_+\|_{\mathrm{TV}}.
    \end{align*}
<!-- end proposition-3 -->
<!-- proof-16: body_md -->
For a test function $f$, write $\partial{\varphi}(f) = \sum_X \partial{\varphi}(X) f(X) = \sum_{\left\{X, Y\right\}} \varphi(X, Y) \left(f(X) - f(Y)\right)$.
    By the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality,
    \begin{align*}
        \ab|\partial{\varphi}(f)|^2 &= \ab(\sum_{\left\{X, Y\right\}} \varphi(X, Y) \left(f(X) - f(Y)\right))^2 \\
        &= \ab(\sum_{\left\{X, Y\right\}} \frac{\varphi(X, Y)}{\sqrt{\mathsf{Cap}(X, Y)}} \sqrt{\mathsf{Cap}(X, Y)} \left(f(X) - f(Y)\right))^2 \\
        &\le A \sum_{\left\{X, Y\right\}} \mathsf{Cap}(X, Y)\left(f(X) - f(Y)\right)^2.
    \end{align*}
    The Dirichlet form on $g = f$ with respect to $K$ is
    \begin{align*}
        \ensuremath{\mathcal{E}}_K(f, f) &= \frac{1}{2} \sum_{X, Y} \pi(X) K(X, Y) \left(f(X) - f(Y)\right)^2 \\
        &= \sum_{\left\{X, Y\right\}} \mathsf{Cap}(X, Y) \left(f(X) - f(Y)\right)^2.
    \end{align*}
    It provides that
    \begin{align*}
        \ab|\partial{\varphi}(f)|^2 \le A \ensuremath{\mathcal{E}}_K(f, f).
    \end{align*}
    Applying the argument on $\ensuremath{\mathcal{E}}_K$ in the proof of \cite[Proposition 6.1]{JMP26}, we have
    \begin{align*}
        \ensuremath{\mathcal{E}}_K\left(K^N g, K^N g\right) \le \frac{1}{2N + 1}
    \end{align*}
    for all observable functions $g$ with $\ab\|g\|_{\infty} \le 1$.
    Therefore,
    \begin{align*}
        \ab\|(\partial{\varphi})K^N\|_{\mathrm{TV}} &= \frac{1}{2}\sup_{g\; :\; \ab\|g\|_{\infty} \le 1} \ab|(\partial{\varphi}) K^N g| \\
        &\le \frac{1}{2} \sqrt{\frac{A}{2N + 1}}.
    \end{align*}

    When $\partial{\varphi} = \mu_- - \mu_+$,
    \begin{align*}
        \mu_- K^N - \mu_+ &= \left(\mu_- - \mu_+\right) K^N + \left(\mu_+ K^N - \mu_+\right) \\
        &= (\partial{\varphi}) K^N + \left(\mu_+ - \pi\right) K^N + \left(\pi - \mu_+\right)
    \end{align*}
    by stationarity of $K$ with respect to $\pi$.
    Then it gives the bound of total variation distance by the triangle inequality as
    \begin{align*}
        \ab\|\mu_- K^N - \mu_+\|_{\mathrm{TV}} \le \ab\|(\partial{\varphi}) K^N\|_{\mathrm{TV}} + 2\ab\|\mu_+ - \pi\|_{\mathrm{TV}}.
    \end{align*}
    Here we use the fact that Markov kernels contract total variation distance.
    Using the above bound, we conclude the lemma.
<!-- end proof-16 -->
<!-- definition-3: body_md -->
\label{def:insertion-kernel}
    The insertion kernel $\widehat{K}_{F, a, \delta}$ is the following procedure.
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item On a valid input $\sigma \in \ensuremath{\mathsf{Good}}\left(F^-\right)$, initialize $X = \mathsf{I}(\sigma)$ and simulate $N$ steps of $K_\beta$.
        \item If the running cost exceeds $\lceil 2N T_{\mathrm{step}}/\delta\rceil$, interrupt the simulation and return a failure symbol $\bot$.
        \item Finally, return $\tau$ if the state is $\mathsf{O}(\tau)$ otherwise return $\bot$.
    \end{enumerate}
<!-- end definition-3 -->
<!-- proof-17: body_md -->
To sample from $\mathcal{F}$, order $\mathcal{F} = \left\{a_1, \ldots, a_m\right\}$ and let $F_i = \left\{a_1, \ldots, a_i\right\}$.
    Set $\delta = \varepsilon/\left(4(m + 1)\right)$.
    We repeatedly try the following procedures until a trial returns an assignment $\sigma \neq \bot$.
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item Draw $\sigma \sim \nu$.
        \item Successively apply $\widehat{K}_{F_i, a_i, \delta}$ for $i = 1, \ldots, m$; if $\bot$ is returned, output $\bot$ and abort this trial.
        \item Return the final assignment with success.
    \end{enumerate}
    By \eqref{eq:insertion-TV-distance} and the union bound, the failure probability of one trial is at most $m\delta \le \varepsilon/4$ and every successful assignment lies in $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Good}}(\mathcal{F})$ and has a desired accuracy.
    Therefore the sampler terminates almost surely and the expected number of trials is at most $4/3$.
    Assume that one trial has a bounded expected cost $\tau$ with failure probability at most $f$ and set $\mathcal{T}$ as the expected running cost until success.
    It holds that
    \begin{align*}
        \mathcal{T} \le \tau + f \mathcal{T} \quad \implies \quad \mathcal{T} \le \frac{\tau}{1 - f}.
    \end{align*}

    It remains to bound $\tau$.
    By the above argument with the choice of $\beta$ and $N$, the expected cost of one insertion is at most
    \begin{align*}
        \ensuremath{\mathrm{poly}}\left(I, \delta^{-1}, K_0, K_2, (1 - \kappa)^{-1}\right)
    \end{align*}
    with an absolute degree.
    By $\delta = \varepsilon/\left(4(m + 1)\right)$ and the fact that there are at most $m$ insertions in a trial,
    \begin{align*}
        \tau \le m \cdot \ensuremath{\mathrm{poly}}\left(I, m, \varepsilon^{-1}, K_0, K_2, (1 - \kappa)^{-1}\right)
    \end{align*}
    which is still polynomial in $I, \varepsilon^{-1}, K_0, K_2$ and $(1 - \kappa)^{-1}$.
    Putting everything together, the sampler outputs an satisfying assignment of $\mathcal{F}$ within TV-distance $\varepsilon$ to $\mu_{\mathcal{F}}$ with expected running time $\ensuremath{\mathrm{poly}}\left(I, \varepsilon^{-1}, K_0, K_2, (1 - \kappa)^{-1}\right)$.
<!-- end proof-17 -->
<!-- lemma-15: body_md -->
\label{lem:connected-set-count}
    In a graph of maximum degree at most $\Delta$, the number of connected sets of size $\ell$ containing a certain vertex is at most $(\mathrm{e} \Delta)^{\ell - 1}$.
<!-- end lemma-15 -->
<!-- proposition-4: body_md -->
\label{prop:root-event}
    Fix $\zeta \in (0, 1)$ such that $L \le r = \floor{q_0 n}$ for $L = \left\lceil \log_2 (32m/\zeta)\right\rceil$.
    Conditional on the structure generation and every choice of bad-variable signs, with probability at least $1 - \zeta$ over the good-variable signs,
    \begin{align} \label{eq:good-root-event}
        \sup_{a \in \mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{good}}}\; \sum_{\gamma \in \ensuremath{\mathsf{CS}}_{\Psi}(a)} q_\gamma \mathrm{e}^{(1 + \tau) \ab|\gamma|} \le \rho.
    \end{align}
<!-- end proposition-4 -->
<!-- proof-18: body_md -->
Write
    \begin{align*}
        A_a \triangleq \sum_{\gamma \in \ensuremath{\mathsf{CS}}_{\Psi}(a), \ab|\gamma| \le L} q_\gamma \mathrm{e}^{(1 + \tau) \ab|\gamma|}, \quad
        T_a \triangleq \sum_{\gamma \in \ensuremath{\mathsf{CS}}_{\Psi}(a), \ab|\gamma| > L} q_\gamma \mathrm{e}^{(1 + \tau) \ab|\gamma|}.
    \end{align*}
    Put $z = \mathrm{e}^2 / C_* < 10^{-3}$.
    By \Cref{lem:expansion,prop:good-property}, for every $S \subseteq \mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{good}}$ with $\ab|S| \le L$, the violating probability is at most
    \begin{align*}
        q_S \le 2^{-(k - 12) \ab|S|} = p_*^{\ab|S|}.
    \end{align*}
    Then with \Cref{lem:connected-set-count}, the contribution from $\ab|\gamma| \le L$ is at most
    \begin{align*}
        A_a \le \sum_{\ell \ge 1} \ab(\mathrm{e} \overline{D})^{\ell - 1} p_*^{\ell} \mathrm{e}^{(1 + \tau)\ell} = \frac{\mathrm{e}}{C_* \cdot \overline{D}(1 - z)}.
    \end{align*}
    For the tail $T_a$ with $\ab|\gamma| > L$, note that for every $S \subseteq \mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{good}}$,
    \begin{align*}
        \E[\text{good signs}]{q_S} \le 2^{-\sum_{c \in S} \ab|\ensuremath{\operatorname{vbl}}^{\mathrm{good}}(c)|} \le p_*^{\ab|S|}.
    \end{align*}
    Therefore
    \begin{align*}
         
    \ensuremath{\mathbb{E}\ifblank{T_a}{}{_{T_a}}\left[#2\right]} 
 \le \sum_{\ell = L + 1}^{\infty} \ab(\mathrm{e} \overline{D})^{\ell - 1} p_*^{\ell} \mathrm{e}^{(1 + \tau) \ell} = \frac{\mathrm{e} z^{L}}{C_* \cdot \overline{D} (1 - z)}.
    \end{align*}
    The Markov inequality gives that
    \begin{align*}
         
    \ensuremath{\mathbb{P}\ifblank{\max_{a \in \mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{good}}} \; T_a > \frac{1}{C_* \cdot \overline{D}}}{}{_{\max_{a \in \mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{good}}} \; T_a > \frac{1}{C_* \cdot \overline{D}}}}\left(#2\right)} 
 \le 3m z^L < \zeta.
    \end{align*}
    Consequently, with probability at least $1 - \zeta$, for every $a \in \mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{good}}$,
    \begin{align*}
        A_a + T_a \le \frac{\mathrm{e}}{C_* \cdot \overline{D}(1 - z)} + \frac{1}{C_* \cdot \overline{D}} \le \rho,
    \end{align*}
    which concludes the proposition.
<!-- end proof-18 -->
<!-- corollary-1: body_md -->
\label{cor:good-fast-sampler}
    On the structural events and \eqref{eq:good-root-event}, there is a callable procedure $\ensuremath{\mathsf{GoodSampler}}(F, \delta)$ for every $F \subseteq \mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{good}}$ and $\delta \in (0, 1)$ that returns a satisfying assignment of $\Psi[F]$ with total variation error at most $\delta$.
    Its expected running cost is bounded by a polynomial of universal degree in the input size $I$ and $\delta^{-1}$.
<!-- end corollary-1 -->
<!-- proof-19: body_md -->
For every $F \subseteq \mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{good}}$ and $a \in F$, for convenience, we use the notation of \Cref{thm:modified-recursive-insertion}.
    By \Cref{lem:core-assignment-good-variable-extension},
    \begin{align*}
        R_S^2 \le (1 - x)^{-2\ab|N_F[S]|} \le \mathrm{e}^{b_0 \ab|S|}, \quad \ell(S) \le \overline{D} \ab|S|
    \end{align*}
    where $b_0 = -2\overline{D} \ln(1 - x) \le 2\mathrm{e}/C_*$.
    On the event \eqref{eq:good-root-event},
    \begin{align*}
        \sum_{S} q_S R_S^2 \le \rho, \quad \sum_{S} q_S R_S^2 \ell(S) \le \overline{D} \rho = \frac{4}{C_*}.
    \end{align*}
    For the pairing moment, $S \cup T$ is connected since $S$ and $T$ are connected subsets containing $a$.
    A fixed union $U$ has at most $3^{\ab|U| - 1}$ ordered representations by such a pair: each clause label in $U \setminus \left\{a\right\}$ can belong to only $S$, only $T$ and both of them.
    Therefore, by \eqref{eq:good-root-event},
    \begin{align*}
        \sum_{S, T} q_{S \cup T} R_S^2 &\le \sum_{\text{connected}\; U \ni a} 3^{\ab|U| - 1} \mathrm{e}^{b_0 \ab|U|} q_U \le \frac{\rho}{3}.
    \end{align*}
    Consequently, by the choice $K_0 = K_2 = 1$ and $\kappa = 1/4$, the hypothesis of \Cref{thm:modified-recursive-insertion} holds.
    Thus there is a sampler $\ensuremath{\mathsf{GoodSampler}}(F, \delta)$ on every input $F \subseteq \mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{good}}$ and $\delta \in (0, 1)$ such that its output law is within TV-distance at most $\delta$ of the uniform distribution over all satisfying assignments of $\Psi[F]$ and its expected running cost is at most $\ensuremath{\mathrm{poly}}\left(I, \delta^{-1}\right)$ with an absolute polynomial degree.
<!-- end proof-19 -->
<!-- theorem-5: body_md -->
\label{thm:core-spectral-gap}
Suppose $n\ge2$ and $k\ge30$, with the parameters defined in
\eqref{eq:core-parameters}. Assume
\eqref{eq:core-feasibility-extension},
\eqref{eq:core-component-growth-input},
\eqref{eq:core-incidence-input},
\eqref{eq:core-dependency-input}, and
\eqref{eq:core-polymer-root}.
If $s\ge1$, then, for every $f:\Omega\to\mathbb R$,
\begin{equation}
 \operatorname{Var}_{\mu^{\mathrm{bad}}}(f)
 \le n\sum_{j=1}^s V_j^{\mu^{\mathrm{bad}}}(f),
 \qquad
 \ensuremath{\mathsf{gap}}(\mathcal{K})\ge\frac1{sn}\ge n^{-2}.
 \label{eq:core-gap}
\end{equation}
Consequently, for every $\sigma_0\in\Omega$ and integer $t\ge0$,
\begin{equation}
 \ab\|\mathcal{K}^t\left(\sigma_0,\cdot\right) - \mu^{\mathrm{bad}}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}
 \le \frac12\,2^{n/2}\exp\left(-\frac{t}{sn}\right).
 \label{eq:core-tv}
\end{equation}
<!-- end theorem-5 -->
<!-- lemma-16: body_md -->
\label{lem-core-cluster-connectivity}
If $a_\Gamma\ne0$, then $\mathcal{H}_\Psi[U_\Gamma]$ is connected.
<!-- end lemma-16 -->
<!-- proof-20: body_md -->
Suppose that $a_\Gamma\ne0$. By \eqref{eq:core-cluster-data}, this implies
$\varphi(\Gamma)\ne0$. If the incompatibility graph $I(\Gamma)$ were
disconnected, there would be no connected spanning subgraph in the sum
defining $\varphi(\Gamma)$ in \eqref{eq:ursell-coefficient}, and hence
$\varphi(\Gamma)=0$, a contradiction. Thus $I(\Gamma)$ is connected.

Each polymer $\gamma_i$ is a connected set of clause labels in
$\mathcal{H}_\Psi$. If $i$ and $j$ are adjacent in $I(\Gamma)$, then
$\gamma_i$ and $\gamma_j$ are incompatible: they either intersect, or
an edge of $\mathcal{H}_\Psi$ joins a clause in $\gamma_i$ to a clause in
$\gamma_j$. In either case, $\gamma_i\cup\gamma_j$ is connected in
$\mathcal{H}_\Psi$. Since $I(\Gamma)$ is connected, following paths in
$I(\Gamma)$ shows that the full union
$U_\Gamma=\bigcup_{i=1}^{\ell}\gamma_i$ is connected in $\mathcal{H}_\Psi$.
<!-- end proof-20 -->
<!-- lemma-17: body_md -->
\label{lem:core-cluster-expansion}
Under \eqref{eq:core-polymer-root},
\begin{equation}
 \ln Z(\sigma)=\sum_\Gamma a_\Gamma Y_\Gamma(\sigma); \qquad \qquad
 \forall\, a\in\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{good}},\quad
 \sum_{\Gamma:a\in U_\Gamma}|a_\Gamma|e^{\tau K_\Gamma}\le\rho.
 \label{eq:core-cluster-root}
\end{equation}
Furthermore, we have \[
 \sum_\Gamma\sup_{\sigma\in\Omega}|a_\Gamma Y_\Gamma(\sigma)|
 \le m\rho<\infty.
\]
Hence, the series $\sum_\Gamma a_\Gamma Y_\Gamma(\sigma)$ converges absolutely
and uniformly for $\sigma\in\Omega$.
<!-- end lemma-17 -->
<!-- proof-21: body_md -->
Apply Theorem~\ref{thm:kotecky-preiss} with
\[
 u_\gamma=q_\gamma e^{\tau|\gamma|},
 \qquad A(\gamma)=|\gamma|.
\]
For every polymer $\gamma$ and every $\sigma\in\Omega$, each
$\chi_a(\sigma)$ belongs to $\{0,1\}$.
Hence, by \eqref{eq:core-polymer-activity},
\[
 |z_\gamma(\sigma)|e^{\tau|\gamma|}
 =q_\gamma e^{\tau|\gamma|}
      \prod_{a\in\gamma}\chi_a(\sigma)
 \le q_\gamma e^{\tau|\gamma|}
 =u_\gamma.
\]
Thus the upper bound $u_\gamma$ is independent of the core
configuration $\sigma$.
Every polymer $\gamma'$ incompatible with $\gamma$ contains at least one
clause in $N_{\mathcal{H}_\Psi}[\gamma]$. Therefore, using
\eqref{eq:core-polymer-root}, \eqref{eq:core-dependency-input} and \eqref{eq:core-parameters},
\begin{align*}
 \sum_{\gamma'\not\sim\gamma}u_{\gamma'}e^{|\gamma'|}=\sum_{\gamma'\not\sim\gamma}
       q_{\gamma'}e^{(1+\tau)|\gamma'|}\le\sum_{a\in N_{\mathcal{H}_\Psi}[\gamma]}
       \sum_{\gamma'\in\ensuremath{\mathsf{CS}}_\Psi(a)}
       q_{\gamma'}e^{(1+\tau)|\gamma'|}\le |N_{\mathcal{H}_\Psi}[\gamma]|\rho
 \le\overline D\rho|\gamma|
 \le|\gamma|.
\end{align*}
Thus
\eqref{eq:kotecky-preiss-condition} holds.

Applying Theorem~\ref{thm:kotecky-preiss} to the partition function
\eqref{eq:core-polymer-partition}, with $w_\gamma=z_\gamma(\sigma)$,
gives the absolutely convergent series
\eqref{eq:polymer-cluster-expansion-prelim}. Thus, for every
$\sigma\in\Omega$,
\begin{equation}
 \ln Z(\sigma)
 =\sum_{\ell\ge1}\frac1{\ell!}
   \sum_{\gamma_1,\ldots,\gamma_\ell}
      \varphi\left(\gamma_1,\ldots,\gamma_\ell\right)
      \prod_{i=1}^{\ell}z_{\gamma_i}(\sigma),
 \label{eq:core-log-polymer-expansion}
\end{equation}
where the inner sum ranges over all ordered tuples of polymers, with
repetitions allowed.

For a tuple $\Gamma=\left(\gamma_1,\ldots,\gamma_\ell\right)$ and $a\in U_\Gamma$,
let $r_a(\Gamma)\triangleq|\{i\in[\ell]:a\in\gamma_i\}|\ge1$.
Since $\chi_a(\sigma)\in\{0,1\}$, the definition of $Y_\Gamma$ in
\eqref{eq:core-cluster-data} gives
\[
 \prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\prod_{a\in\gamma_i}\chi_a(\sigma)
 =\prod_{a\in U_\Gamma}\chi_a(\sigma)^{r_a(\Gamma)}
 =\prod_{a\in U_\Gamma}\chi_a(\sigma)
 =Y_\Gamma(\sigma).
\]
Substituting the activities from \eqref{eq:core-polymer-activity}
and using the definition of $a_\Gamma$ in
\eqref{eq:core-cluster-data}, we obtain
\begin{align*}
 \frac{\varphi(\Gamma)}{\ell!}
      \prod_{i=1}^{\ell}z_{\gamma_i}(\sigma)
 =\frac{\varphi(\Gamma)}{\ell!}
      \left(\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}(-1)^{|\gamma_i|}q_{\gamma_i}\right)
      \left(\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\prod_{a\in\gamma_i}\chi_a(\sigma)\right)=a_\Gamma Y_\Gamma(\sigma).
\end{align*}
Consequently, \eqref{eq:core-log-polymer-expansion} becomes
\[
 \ln Z(\sigma)
 =\sum_{\ell\ge1}\sum_{\Gamma=\left(\gamma_1,\ldots,\gamma_\ell\right)}
      a_\Gamma Y_\Gamma(\sigma)
 =\sum_\Gamma a_\Gamma Y_\Gamma(\sigma),
\]
which proves the expansion asserted in \eqref{eq:core-cluster-root}.

For every fixed polymer $\gamma_0$, the estimate
\eqref{eq:kotecky-preiss-pinned}, with $A\left(\gamma_0\right)=|\gamma_0|$, gives
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{\ell\ge1}\frac1{(\ell-1)!}
  \sum_{\gamma_2,\ldots,\gamma_\ell}
   |\varphi\left(\gamma_0,\gamma_2,\ldots,\gamma_\ell\right)|
   \prod_{i=2}^{\ell}u_{\gamma_i}
 \le e^{|\gamma_0|}.
 \label{eq:core-pinned-majorant}
\end{equation}
Fix a clause $a\in\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{good}}$ and put
\begin{equation}
 S_a\triangleq
 \sum_{\Gamma:a\in U_\Gamma}|a_\Gamma|e^{\tau K_\Gamma}.
 \label{eq:core-clause-root-sum}
\end{equation}
By \eqref{eq:core-cluster-data} and the definition of $u_\gamma$,
every tuple $\Gamma=\left(\gamma_1,\ldots,\gamma_\ell\right)$ satisfies
\[
 |a_\Gamma|e^{\tau K_\Gamma}
 =\frac{|\varphi(\Gamma)|}{\ell!}
      \prod_{i=1}^{\ell}u_{\gamma_i}.
\]
Also, $a\in U_\Gamma$ if and only if $a\in\gamma_i$ for at least one
position $i$. 
Thus,
\[
 \mathbbm{1}\left[a\in U_\Gamma\right]
 \le\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\mathbbm{1}\left[a\in\gamma_i\right].
\]
The right-hand side counts every position whose polymer contains $a$,
including distinct occurrences of the same polymer. Since all terms
below are nonnegative, this gives
\begin{align}\label{eq:core-clause-root-marking}
 S_a=\sum_{\ell\ge1}\frac1{\ell!}
    \sum_{\gamma_1,\ldots,\gamma_\ell}
      \mathbbm{1}\left[a\in U_\Gamma\right]|\varphi(\Gamma)|
      \prod_{i=1}^{\ell}u_{\gamma_i}\le\sum_{\ell\ge1}\frac1{\ell!}
    \sum_{\gamma_1,\ldots,\gamma_\ell}
      \left(\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\mathbbm{1}\left[a\in\gamma_i\right]\right)
      |\varphi(\Gamma)|\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}u_{\gamma_i}.
\end{align}
For $\ell\ge1$ and $i\in[\ell]$, define the sum associated with
position $i$ by
\[
 T_{\ell,i}(a)\triangleq
 \sum_{\gamma_1,\ldots,\gamma_\ell}
    \mathbbm{1}\left[a\in\gamma_i\right]
    |\varphi\left(\gamma_1,\ldots,\gamma_\ell\right)|
    \prod_{j=1}^{\ell}u_{\gamma_j}.
\]
Swapping positions $i$ and $1$ bijectively maps the tuples with
$a\in\gamma_i$ to those with $a\in\gamma_1$, while preserving
the Ursell coefficient and the product $\prod_j u_{\gamma_j}$. Hence,
\[
 T_{\ell,i}(a)=T_{\ell,1}(a)
 \qquad\text{for every }\ell\ge1\text{ and }i\in[\ell].
\]
Writing the right-hand side of \eqref{eq:core-clause-root-marking}
in terms of these sums therefore gives
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 S_a
 \le\sum_{\ell\ge1}\frac1{\ell!}
        \sum_{i=1}^{\ell}T_{\ell,i}(a)=\sum_{\ell\ge1}\frac{\ell}{\ell!}T_{\ell,1}(a)
  =\sum_{\ell\ge1}\frac1{(\ell-1)!}T_{\ell,1}(a).
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:core-first-position-sum}
\end{equation}
In $T_{\ell,1}(a)$, rename the first polymer $\gamma_1$ as $\gamma_0$
and factor out $u_{\gamma_0}$. This yields
\[
 T_{\ell,1}(a)
 =\sum_{\gamma_0:\,a\in\gamma_0}u_{\gamma_0}
   \sum_{\gamma_2,\ldots,\gamma_\ell}
    |\varphi\left(\gamma_0,\gamma_2,\ldots,\gamma_\ell\right)|
    \prod_{j=2}^{\ell}u_{\gamma_j}.
\]
Substituting this identity into \eqref{eq:core-first-position-sum}
