 \label{eq:core-lifted-pair-swap-invariance}
\end{equation}
Moreover, for every $e\in\mathcal J$,
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \widetilde\eta_e\vee\widetilde\xi_e
 &=\begin{cases}
   \xi_e\vee\eta_e,&V_e\subseteq D,\\
   \eta_e\vee\xi_e,&V_e\not\subseteq D,
  \end{cases}\\
 &=\eta_e\vee\xi_e.
 \end{aligned}
\]
Thus,
\[
 \{e\in\mathcal J:\widetilde\eta_e\vee\widetilde\xi_e=1\}
 =\{e\in\mathcal J:\eta_e\vee\xi_e=1\}.
\]
Finally, for every $\sigma,\omega\in\Omega$ and
$\eta,\xi\in\{0,1\}^{\mathcal J}$, applying
\eqref{eq:core-swap-map} twice gives
\[
 T_D\Bigl(T_D\bigl((\sigma,\eta),(\omega,\xi)\bigr)\Bigr)
 =\bigl((\sigma,\eta),(\omega,\xi)\bigr).
\]
Indeed, each selected coordinate and auxiliary bit is swapped twice,
returning to its original value, while all others remain unchanged.
Moreover, the edge indicators $\eta_e\vee\xi_e$ are unchanged by
$T_D$ for every input pair and every fixed $D$. Thus, for each fixed
$g$ and each $D$ that is a union of components of $g$, the restriction
of $T_D$ to the fiber of pairs whose hypergraph is $g$ is a bijection
from that fiber to itself. By
\eqref{eq:core-lifted-pair-swap-invariance}, this restricted bijection
preserves the pair probabilities.


For each $C\in\operatorname{Comp}(g)$, independently draw a fresh bit
$\varepsilon_C\in\{0,1\}$ with
\[
 \mathbb P_\varepsilon\left(\varepsilon_C=0\right)
 =\mathbb P_\varepsilon\left(\varepsilon_C=1\right)=\frac12.
\]
Collect these bits into the vector
$\varepsilon=\left(\varepsilon_C\right)_{C\in\operatorname{Comp}(g)}
\in\{0,1\}^{\operatorname{Comp}(g)}$ and define its complementary bit
vector by
\[
 \bar\varepsilon=\left(1-\varepsilon_C\right)_{C\in\operatorname{Comp}(g)}
 \in\{0,1\}^{\operatorname{Comp}(g)}.
\]
Define
\[
 D(\varepsilon)=
 \bigcup_{\substack{C\in\operatorname{Comp}(g)\\\varepsilon_C=1}}C
\]
and apply $T_{D(\varepsilon)}$ to this sample pair.
By definition, $D(\varepsilon)$ is a union of connected components of the
associated hypergraph $g$, so it satisfies the condition in \eqref{eq:core-swap-region}.
Thus a component $C$ is swapped in its entirety if $\varepsilon_C=1$
and is left unchanged if $\varepsilon_C=0$.
Let $X^\varepsilon,\left(X'\right)^\varepsilon$ be the two
resulting configurations.
Since every component is swapped in exactly one of $\varepsilon$ and
$\bar\varepsilon$, we have
$X^{\bar\varepsilon}=\left(X'\right)^\varepsilon$.
The notation $\mathbb E_\varepsilon$ and
$\operatorname{Var}_\varepsilon$ denotes expectation and variance over
$\varepsilon$ alone, with the sample pair
$\bigl((X,\eta),\left(X',\xi\right)\bigr)$ held fixed.
The map $\varepsilon\mapsto\bar\varepsilon$ is a bijection of
$\{0,1\}^{\operatorname{Comp}(g)}$, on which $\varepsilon$ is uniform.
Thus $\varepsilon$ and $\bar\varepsilon$ have the same distribution, as do
$f\left(X^\varepsilon\right)$ and $f\left(X^{\bar\varepsilon}\right)$; in particular, they
have the same mean and variance.
Since $\mathbb E_\varepsilon[f\left(X^\varepsilon\right)]
=\mathbb E_\varepsilon[f\left(X^{\bar\varepsilon}\right)]$, we have
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 f\left(X^\varepsilon\right)-f\left(X^{\bar\varepsilon}\right)
 &=\bigl(f\left(X^\varepsilon\right)
          -\mathbb E_\varepsilon[f\left(X^\varepsilon\right)]\bigr) -\bigl(f\left(X^{\bar\varepsilon}\right)
          -\mathbb E_\varepsilon[f\left(X^{\bar\varepsilon}\right)]\bigr).
 \end{aligned}
\]
Applying $(u-v)^2\le2u^2+2v^2$ to these two mean-subtracted terms
and taking expectation over $\varepsilon$ gives
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E_\varepsilon
   \left(f\left(X^\varepsilon\right)-f\left(X^{\bar\varepsilon}\right)\right)^2
 &\le2\mathbb E_\varepsilon\!\left[
   \bigl(f\left(X^\varepsilon\right)-\mathbb E_\varepsilon[f\left(X^\varepsilon\right)]\bigr)^2
   \right] +2\mathbb E_\varepsilon\!\left[
   \bigl(f\left(X^{\bar\varepsilon}\right)
          -\mathbb E_\varepsilon[f\left(X^{\bar\varepsilon}\right)]\bigr)^2
   \right] \\
 &=2\operatorname{Var}_\varepsilon\left(f\left(X^\varepsilon\right)\right)
   +2\operatorname{Var}_\varepsilon\left(f\left(X^{\bar\varepsilon}\right)\right) =4\operatorname{Var}_\varepsilon\left(f\left(X^\varepsilon\right)\right).
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:core-swap-antithetic}
\end{equation}
For each $C\in\operatorname{Comp}(g)$, define the bit vector
$\varepsilon^C\in\{0,1\}^{\operatorname{Comp}(g)}$ by
\[
\forall C'\in\operatorname{Comp}(g), \qquad
 \left(\varepsilon^C\right)_{C'}=
 \begin{cases}
  1-\varepsilon_C,&C'=C,\\
  \varepsilon_{C'},&C'\ne C.
 \end{cases}
\]
Thus $\varepsilon^C$ differs from $\varepsilon$ only at the bit indexed
by $C$. This changes whether the entire component $C$ is swapped,
while leaving the choices for all other components unchanged.
Write $\varepsilon_{-C}=\left(\varepsilon_{C'}\right)_{C'\in
\operatorname{Comp}(g)\setminus\{C\}}$ for all the other bits.
Since the bits are independent, applying
Lemma~\ref{lem:efron-stein} in Section~\ref{subsec:efron-stein} to the
function $\varepsilon\mapsto f\left(X^\varepsilon\right)$ gives
\begin{equation}
 \operatorname{Var}_\varepsilon\left(f\left(X^\varepsilon\right)\right)
 \le\sum_{C\in\operatorname{Comp}(g)}
     \mathbb E_\varepsilon\!\left[
       \operatorname{Var}_\varepsilon\left(f\left(X^\varepsilon\right)
                                     \mid\varepsilon_{-C}\right)\right].
 \label{eq:core-swap-conditional-variance}
\end{equation}
For each fixed value of $\varepsilon_{-C}$, let $a$ and $b$ denote the
values of $f\left(X^\varepsilon\right)$ when $\varepsilon_C=0$ and
$\varepsilon_C=1$, respectively. Since each choice of $\varepsilon_C$
has probability $1/2$, the conditional mean of $f\left(X^\varepsilon\right)$ is
$(a+b)/2$, and its conditional variance is
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \operatorname{Var}_\varepsilon\left(f\left(X^\varepsilon\right)\mid\varepsilon_{-C}\right)
 &=\frac12\left(a-\frac{a+b}{2}\right)^2
   +\frac12\left(b-\frac{a+b}{2}\right)^2 =\frac{(a-b)^2}{4}.
 \end{aligned}
\]
Flipping $\varepsilon_C$ changes the function value from $a$ to $b$
if $\varepsilon_C=0$, and from $b$ to $a$ if $\varepsilon_C=1$.
Thus $\left(f\left(X^\varepsilon\right)-f\left(X^{\varepsilon^C}\right)\right)^2=(a-b)^2$ for either
choice of $\varepsilon_C$. It follows that
\[
 \operatorname{Var}_\varepsilon\left(f\left(X^\varepsilon\right)\mid\varepsilon_{-C}\right)
 =\frac14\mathbb E_\varepsilon\!\left[
   \left(f\left(X^\varepsilon\right)-f\left(X^{\varepsilon^C}\right)\right)^2
   \mid\varepsilon_{-C}\right].
\]
Taking expectations gives
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 &\mathbb E_\varepsilon\!\left[
   \operatorname{Var}_\varepsilon\left(
     f\left(X^\varepsilon\right)\mid\varepsilon_{-C}\right)\right] \\
 &\quad=\mathbb E_{\varepsilon_{-C}}\!\left[
   \operatorname{Var}_\varepsilon\left(
     f\left(X^\varepsilon\right)\mid\varepsilon_{-C}\right)\right] \\
 &\quad=\frac14\mathbb E_{\varepsilon_{-C}}\!\left[
   \mathbb E_\varepsilon\!\left[
     \left(f\left(X^\varepsilon\right)
           -f\left(X^{\varepsilon^C}\right)\right)^2
     \mid\varepsilon_{-C}\right]\right] \\
 &\quad=\frac14\mathbb E_\varepsilon\!\left[
     \left(f\left(X^\varepsilon\right)
           -f\left(X^{\varepsilon^C}\right)\right)^2\right].
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:core-swap-averaged-bit-variance}
\end{equation}

By \eqref{eq:core-swap-averaged-bit-variance} and \eqref{eq:core-swap-conditional-variance},
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 4\operatorname{Var}_\varepsilon\left(f\left(X^\varepsilon\right)\right)
 &\le4\sum_{C\in\operatorname{Comp}(g)}
     \mathbb E_\varepsilon\!\left[
       \operatorname{Var}_\varepsilon\left(
         f\left(X^\varepsilon\right)\mid\varepsilon_{-C}\right)\right]\\
 &=\sum_{C\in\operatorname{Comp}(g)}
     \mathbb E_\varepsilon\!\left[
       \left(f\left(X^\varepsilon\right)
             -f\left(X^{\varepsilon^C}\right)\right)^2\right].
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:core-swap-efron-stein}
\end{equation} 

Let $Y=\left((X,\eta),(X',\xi)\right)$, and let $G$ be its associated
labelled hypergraph. For every $g$ with $\mathbb P(G=g)>0$ and every fixed
$\varepsilon\in\{0,1\}^{\operatorname{Comp}(g)}$, the set $D(\varepsilon)$
is a union of components of $g$. Thus $T_{D(\varepsilon)}$ is a bijection
on the fiber of sample pairs whose hypergraph is $g$, and
\eqref{eq:core-lifted-pair-swap-invariance} shows that this map leaves
the product probabilities
$\widehat\pi(X,\eta)\widehat\pi\left(X',\xi\right)$ unchanged. Since
conditioning on $G=g$ amounts to normalizing these probabilities on that
fiber, for every sample-pair value $y$ in the fiber we have
\[
 \mathbb P\left(T_{D(\varepsilon)}(Y)=y\mid G=g\right)
 =\mathbb P\left(Y=y\mid G=g\right).
\]
Conditional on $G=g$, the bit vector $\varepsilon$ is sampled
uniformly from $\{0,1\}^{\operatorname{Comp}(g)}$, independently of
$\left(\left(X,\eta\right),\left(X',\xi\right)\right)$.
Consequently, for every
$\sigma,\omega\in\Omega$,
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 &\mathbb P\left(X^\varepsilon=\sigma,
                 \left(X'\right)^\varepsilon=\omega
                 \mid G=g,\varepsilon\right) =\mathbb P\left(X=\sigma,X'=\omega\mid G=g\right).
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:core-conditional-swapped-pair-law}
\end{equation}
By the law of total probability and the independence of $X$ and
$X'$, for every $\sigma,\omega\in\Omega$,
\begin{equation}
 \mathbb P\left(X^\varepsilon=\sigma,
                \left(X'\right)^\varepsilon=\omega\right)
 =\mathbb P\left(X=\sigma,X'=\omega\right)
 =\pi(\sigma)\pi(\omega).
 \label{eq:core-swapped-pair-law}
\end{equation}
Using $X^{\bar\varepsilon}=\left(X'\right)^\varepsilon$,
\eqref{eq:core-swapped-pair-law}, and the independence of $X$ and
$X'$ with common law $\pi$, we obtain
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 &\mathbb E\!\left[\mathbb E_\varepsilon\!\left[
   \left(f\left(X^\varepsilon\right)
         -f\left(X^{\bar\varepsilon}\right)\right)^2\right]\right] 
 =\mathbb E\!\left[\mathbb E_\varepsilon\!\left[
   \left(f\left(X^\varepsilon\right)
         -f\left(\left(X'\right)^\varepsilon\right)\right)^2\right]\right] \\
 =&\mathbb E\!\left[
   \left(f(X)-f\left(X'\right)\right)^2\right]
   =2\operatorname{Var}_\pi(f).
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:core-independent-pair-variance}
\end{equation}
We now average over the independent lifted samples
$(X,\eta),\left(X',\xi\right)\sim\widehat\pi$.
In the following inequality, the outer $\mathbb E$ averages over
$X,\eta,X',\xi$, whereas the inner $\mathbb E_\varepsilon$ averages
only over the swap bits, holding the entire sample pair fixed.
Combining \eqref{eq:core-swap-antithetic} and
\eqref{eq:core-swap-efron-stein}, taking these expectations, and using
\eqref{eq:core-independent-pair-variance} gives
\begin{equation*}
 \operatorname{Var}_\pi(f)
 \le\frac12\mathbb E\!\left[
   \sum_{C\in\operatorname{Comp}(g)}\mathbb E_\varepsilon\!\left[
     \left(f\left(X^\varepsilon\right)
           -f\left(X^{\varepsilon^C}\right)\right)^2\right]\right].
\end{equation*}
<!-- end proof-31 -->
<!-- proof-32: body_md -->
Fix $\Gamma\in\Lambda$ satisfying $J_\Gamma\cap A\ne\emptyset$, and
choose $i\in J_\Gamma\cap A$. By \eqref{eq:core-geometric-scope},
$B_i\cap\ensuremath{\operatorname{vbl}}(U_\Gamma)\ne\emptyset$. Since
$U_\Gamma=\bigcup_{r=1}^{\ell}\gamma_r$ by
\eqref{eq:core-cluster-data}, there exists $a\in U_\Gamma$ such that
$\ensuremath{\operatorname{vbl}}(a)\cap B_i\ne\emptyset$. Each polymer $\gamma_r$ is a subset of
$\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{good}}$, so $U_\Gamma\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{good}}$ and hence
$a\in\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{good}}$. Since $i\in A$ and $B_i\subseteq W_A$, this clause
satisfies $\ensuremath{\operatorname{vbl}}(a)\cap W_A\ne\emptyset$. Hence,
\[
 R(A)
 \le\sum_{\substack{a\in\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{good}}\\
                      \ensuremath{\operatorname{vbl}}(a)\cap W_A\ne\emptyset}}
       \sum_{\substack{\Gamma\in\Lambda\\a\in U_\Gamma}}
       |a_\Gamma|e^{\tau K_\Gamma}.
\]
The right-hand side may count the same $\Gamma$ once for each
clause in $U_\Gamma$ incident to $W_A$, which only increases the sum.
For each $a\in\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{good}}$, \eqref{eq:core-cluster-root} gives
\[
 \sum_{\substack{\Gamma\in\Lambda\\a\in U_\Gamma}}
       |a_\Gamma|e^{\tau K_\Gamma}
 \le\sum_{\Gamma:a\in U_\Gamma}
       |a_\Gamma|e^{\tau K_\Gamma}
 \le\rho.
\]
Therefore,
\[
 R(A)
 \le\rho\left|\left\{a\in\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{good}}:
       \ensuremath{\operatorname{vbl}}(a)\cap W_A\ne\emptyset\right\}\right|.
\]
Since $\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{good}}\subseteq\mathcal{C}$, the definition
\eqref{eq:incident-clause-count} gives
\[
 \left|\left\{a\in\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{good}}:
       \ensuremath{\operatorname{vbl}}(a)\cap W_A\ne\emptyset\right\}\right|
 \le I(W_A).
\]
Hence, $R(A)\le\rho I(W_A)$. The lemma is proved.
<!-- end proof-32 -->
<!-- proof-33: body_md -->
Recall that $\zeta$ is a hypergraph on vertex set $[s]$, and that the
hyperedge indexed by a retained label $\Gamma$ has vertex set $J_\Gamma$.
Suppose that $\Gamma\in\mathcal D_t$ and $\zeta_\Gamma=1$. By the choice
of $a_t$, there is some $i\in\mathcal R_t$ such that
$B_i\cap\ensuremath{\operatorname{vbl}}(a_t)\ne\emptyset$. Since $a_t\in U_\Gamma$, the definition
of $J_\Gamma$ in \eqref{eq:core-geometric-scope} gives
$i\in J_\Gamma$. Thus the retained hyperedge $J_\Gamma$ meets
$\mathcal R_t$, and adding its vertices preserves connection to $j$.
It follows inductively that every vertex in $\mathcal R_Q$ is connected
to $j$ in the hypergraph $\zeta$, and hence $\mathcal R_Q\subseteq\mathcal A_j$.

Conversely, consider any hyperedge $J_\Gamma$ of $\zeta$ meeting
$\mathcal R_Q$. By the definition of $J_\Gamma$, some clause
$a\in U_\Gamma$ contains a variable from a block $B_i$ with
$i\in\mathcal R_Q$. Before the procedure stops, every such good clause
is used. When $a$ is used, the label $\Gamma$ is checked, unless it was
checked earlier; since $\zeta_\Gamma=1$, all vertices of $J_\Gamma$ are
therefore added to $\mathcal R_Q$. Thus every retained hyperedge that
meets $\mathcal R_Q$ is entirely contained in $\mathcal R_Q$.
Consequently, no path in $\zeta$ can connect a vertex of $\mathcal R_Q$
to a vertex outside $\mathcal R_Q$. Since $j\in\mathcal R_Q$, this proves
$\mathcal A_j\subseteq\mathcal R_Q$, and therefore
$\mathcal R_Q=\mathcal A_j$.
<!-- end proof-33 -->
<!-- proof-34: body_md -->
For $t<Q$, let $\mathscr F_t$ denote the information revealed before
step $t$. Then $\mathcal D_t$ is $\mathscr F_t$-measurable and
\begin{equation}
 \delta T_t\triangleq T_{t+1}-T_t
 =\sum_{\Gamma\in\mathcal D_t}K_\Gamma\zeta_\Gamma.
 \label{eq:core-exploration-increment}
\end{equation}
Because the indicators in \eqref{eq:core-zeta-law} are independent and
$\mathcal D_t\subseteq\Lambda\setminus\mathcal E_t$, conditionally on
$\mathscr F_t$ the variables
$(\zeta_\Gamma)_{\Gamma\in\mathcal D_t}$ are independent and have the
same Bernoulli laws as in \eqref{eq:core-zeta-law}. 
Define
$p_\Gamma \triangleq 1-e^{-2|a_\Gamma|}$. 
By \eqref{eq:core-zeta-law} and the
conditional independence just noted,
\begin{align*}
 \mathbb E\!\left[e^{\delta T_t/2}\mid\mathscr F_t\right]
 &=\prod_{\Gamma\in\mathcal D_t}
   \mathbb E\!\left[e^{K_\Gamma\zeta_\Gamma/2}
                    \mid\mathscr F_t\right]=\prod_{\Gamma\in\mathcal D_t}
   \left((1-p_\Gamma)+p_\Gamma e^{K_\Gamma/2}\right)=\prod_{\Gamma\in\mathcal D_t}
   \left(1+p_\Gamma\left(e^{K_\Gamma/2}-1\right)\right).
\end{align*}
Applying $1+x\le e^x$ and $e^{K_\Gamma/2}-1\le e^{K_\Gamma/2}$, we obtain
\begin{align*}
 \mathbb E\!\left[e^{\delta T_t/2}\mid\mathscr F_t\right]
 &\le\exp\left(
   \sum_{\Gamma\in\mathcal D_t}
      p_\Gamma\left(e^{K_\Gamma/2}-1\right)\right)\le\exp\left(
   \sum_{\Gamma\in\mathcal D_t}
      p_\Gamma e^{K_\Gamma/2}\right).
\end{align*}
Moreover,
$p_\Gamma=1-e^{-2|a_\Gamma|}\leq 2|a_\Gamma|$.
Therefore,
\begin{align*}
 \mathbb E\!\left[e^{\delta T_t/2}\mid\mathscr F_t\right]
 \le\exp\left(
   2\sum_{\Gamma\in\mathcal D_t}
       |a_\Gamma|e^{K_\Gamma/2}\right).
\end{align*}
Finally, $\tau\ge\ln2>1/2$ by \eqref{eq:core-polymer-root}, so
$e^{K_\Gamma/2}\le e^{\tau K_\Gamma}$. Moreover,
$\mathcal D_t\subseteq\{\Gamma:a_t\in U_\Gamma\}$. 
Combining these two observations with 
\eqref{eq:core-cluster-root}, we obtain
\begin{align*}
 \sum_{\Gamma\in\mathcal D_t}|a_\Gamma|e^{K_\Gamma/2}
 &\le\sum_{\Gamma:a_t\in U_\Gamma}
          |a_\Gamma|e^{\tau K_\Gamma}
 \le\rho.
\end{align*}
Therefore,
\[
 \mathbb E\!\left[e^{\delta T_t/2}\mid\mathscr F_t\right]
 \le e^{2\rho}.
\]
Equivalently,
\begin{equation}
 \mathbb E\!\left[
   e^{\delta T_t/2-2\rho}\mid\mathscr F_t\right]\le1,
 \qquad t<Q.
 \label{eq:core-one-step-supermartingale}
\end{equation}
Here $t\wedge Q=\min\{t,Q\}$. Define the stopped process
\begin{equation}
 M_t=\exp\left(T_{t\wedge Q}/2-2\rho(t\wedge Q)\right),
 \qquad t\ge0,
 \label{eq:core-stopped-supermartingale}
\end{equation}
with respect to the exploration filtration. On the event $t<Q$, we have
\[
 M_{t+1}=M_t e^{\delta T_t/2-2\rho}.
\]
Since $M_t$ is determined by the information revealed before step $t$,
it is $\mathscr F_t$-measurable. We may therefore take it outside the
conditional expectation. Using
\eqref{eq:core-one-step-supermartingale}, on the event $t<Q$ we obtain
\begin{align*}
 \mathbb E[M_{t+1}\mid\mathscr F_t]
 &=M_t\,
   \mathbb E\!\left[e^{\delta T_t/2-2\rho}\mid\mathscr F_t\right]\le M_t.
\end{align*}
On the event $t\ge Q$, the process has already stopped, so
$M_{t+1}=M_t$. Thus $(M_t)_{t\ge0}$ is a nonnegative supermartingale.

Finally, each of the $Q$ exploration steps uses a distinct clause
$a_t\in\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{good}}$. Hence
$Q\le|\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{good}}|\le|\mathcal{C}|=m$. It follows that $m\wedge Q=Q$, while
$T_Q=T$. Thus \eqref{eq:core-stopped-supermartingale} gives
$M_m=e^{T/2-2\rho Q}$. Moreover, the supermartingale property gives
$\mathbb E M_m\le\mathbb E M_0$. Therefore
\begin{equation*}
 \mathbb E e^{T/2-2\rho Q}
 =\mathbb E M_m\le\mathbb E M_0=M_0=1.
\end{equation*} 
The lemma is proved.
<!-- end proof-34 -->
<!-- proof-35: body_md -->
By
\eqref{eq:core-component-growth-input}, every $v\in W\cap\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{high}}$ satisfies
$\deg(v)\ge\Delta$. Hence,
\[
 \Delta|W\cap\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{high}}|
 \le\sum_{v\in W\cap\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{high}}}\deg(v).
\]
By the definition of $I(W)$ in \eqref{eq:incident-clause-count}, exactly
$I(W)$ clauses contain at least one variable of $W$. Each such clause
contains at most $k$ variables and therefore contributes at most $k$ to
the degree sum. Hence
\[
 \sum_{v\in W\cap\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{high}}}\deg(v)\le kI(W).
\]
Therefore, 
\[
  |W\cap\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{high}}|\le\frac{k}{\Delta}I(W).
\]
Combining this with 
\eqref{eq:core-component-growth-input} gives
\[
 |W|\le\frac{k}{2}|W\cap\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{high}}|
 \le\frac{k^2}{2\Delta}I(W).
\]
The lemma is proved.
<!-- end proof-35 -->
<!-- proof-36: body_md -->
Recall that $\mathcal{H}_\Psi$ is the dependency graph of the residual formula
$\Psi$ on the clause labels $\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{good}}$, where two clause labels are
adjacent exactly when their residual clauses share a good variable.
Recall also that $\mathcal{G}_\Phi$ is the primal graph of the original formula
$\Phi$: its vertices are the variables of $\Phi$, and two variables are
adjacent when they occur together in an original clause.


Fix
$\Gamma\in\mathcal E_Q$ with $\zeta_\Gamma=1$. Recall that
$\Gamma=(\gamma_1,\ldots,\gamma_\ell)$ is a tuple of polymers, each
$\gamma_i$ is a connected set of clause labels in $\mathcal{H}_\Psi$, and
$U_\Gamma=\bigcup_{i=1}^{\ell}\gamma_i$ is itself a set of clause labels. 
Since $\zeta_\Gamma=1$,
\eqref{eq:core-zeta-law} forces $a_\Gamma\ne0$: indeed, if
$a_\Gamma=0$, then
$\mathbb P_\zeta(\zeta_\Gamma=1)=1-e^{-2|a_\Gamma|}=0$.
By \Cref{lem-core-cluster-connectivity}, the induced clause graph
$\mathcal{H}_\Psi[U_\Gamma]$ is connected.

Choose a spanning tree $T_\Gamma$ of $\mathcal{H}_\Psi[U_\Gamma]$. For each edge
$e=\{c,d\}$ of $T_\Gamma$, choose a good variable $x_e$ appearing in
both original clauses $C_c$ and $C_d$; such a variable exists by the
definition of $\mathcal{H}_\Psi$. Define
\begin{equation}
 L_\Gamma=
 \left(\bigcup_{c\in U_\Gamma}
       \bigl(\ensuremath{\operatorname{vbl}}(c)\cap\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{bad}}\bigr)\right)
 \cup\{x_e:e\in E(T_\Gamma)\}
 \cup W_{J_\Gamma}.
 \label{eq:core-label-variable-set}
\end{equation}
For each $c\in U_\Gamma$, the connector variables $x_e$ on tree edges
incident to $c$, together with the bad variables of $C_c$, lie in the
same clause $C_c$ and hence are mutually adjacent in $\mathcal{G}_\Phi$. Since
$T_\Gamma$ is connected, the union of the first two sets in
\eqref{eq:core-label-variable-set} is connected.

For every $i\in J_\Gamma$, \eqref{eq:core-geometric-scope} gives
$B_i\cap\ensuremath{\operatorname{vbl}}(U_\Gamma)\ne\emptyset$. Thus $B_i$ intersects the first
set in \eqref{eq:core-label-variable-set}. Since $B_i$ is itself
connected in $\mathcal{G}_\Phi$, adjoining the blocks
$\{B_i:i\in J_\Gamma\}$ to the union of the first two sets in
\eqref{eq:core-label-variable-set} preserves connectivity. By
\eqref{eq:core-block-union}, the resulted set
is exactly $L_\Gamma$.
Hence, $L_\Gamma$ is connected. 
Moreover, the label $\Gamma$
belongs to a unique $\mathcal D_t$. By
\eqref{eq:core-exploration-new-labels}
and the choice of $a_t$, we have $a_t\in U_\Gamma$. 
Furthermore, by the
exploration rule, $a_t$ intersects some currently discovered block
$B_i$ with $i\in\mathcal R_t$, and hence
$\ensuremath{\operatorname{vbl}}(a_t)\cap W_{\mathcal R_t}\ne\emptyset$ by
\eqref{eq:core-block-union}.
Since $a_t\in U_\Gamma$ and
$W_{\mathcal R_t}\subseteq\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{bad}}$, we have
\[
 \emptyset\ne\ensuremath{\operatorname{vbl}}(a_t)\cap W_{\mathcal R_t}
 \subseteq W_{\mathcal R_t}\cap
 \bigcup_{c\in U_\Gamma}\bigl(\ensuremath{\operatorname{vbl}}(c)\cap\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{bad}}\bigr).
\]
Combined with
\eqref{eq:core-label-variable-set}, we have
\begin{equation}
 L_\Gamma\cap W_{\mathcal R_t}\ne\emptyset.
 \label{eq:core-label-set-meets-current-blocks}
\end{equation}

Set $L_0=B_j$ and, for
$0\le t<Q$, define
\begin{align}\label{eq-def-ltplusone}
L_{t+1}=L_t\cup
 \bigcup_{\substack{\Gamma\in\mathcal D_t\\
                     \zeta_\Gamma=1}}L_\Gamma.  
\end{align}
We prove by induction that $L_t$ is connected and contains
$W_{\mathcal R_t}$. At $t=0$, we have
$L_0=B_j=W_{\mathcal R_0}$ because $\mathcal R_0=\{j\}$. Now suppose
that $L_t$ is connected and $W_{\mathcal R_t}\subseteq L_t$. Then every
set adjoined in the definition of $L_{t+1}$ intersects
$W_{\mathcal R_t}$ by
\eqref{eq:core-label-set-meets-current-blocks}, so $L_{t+1}$ is
connected because $W_{\mathcal R_t}\subseteq L_t$. 
By \eqref{eq:core-label-variable-set}, we have 
\begin{align}\label{eq-wjgammasubseteqlgamma}
\forall\  \Gamma,\qquad    W_{J_\Gamma}
 \subseteq L_\Gamma. 
\end{align}
Moreover, by the
update rule for $\mathcal R_{t+1}$,
\[
 W_{\mathcal R_{t+1}}
 =W_{\mathcal R_t}\cup
   \bigcup_{\substack{\Gamma\in\mathcal D_t\\
                       \zeta_\Gamma=1}}W_{J_\Gamma}.
\]
Combined with \eqref{eq-def-ltplusone}, \eqref{eq-wjgammasubseteqlgamma}, and $W_{\mathcal R_t}\subseteq L_t$,
we obtain
\[
 W_{\mathcal R_{t+1}}
 =W_{\mathcal R_t}\cup
   \bigcup_{\substack{\Gamma\in\mathcal D_t\\
                       \zeta_\Gamma=1}}W_{J_\Gamma}
 \subseteq L_t\cup
 \bigcup_{\substack{\Gamma\in\mathcal D_t\\
                     \zeta_\Gamma=1}}L_\Gamma = L_{t+1}.
\]
Thus $L_Q$ is connected and contains
$W_{\mathcal R_Q}=W_{\mathcal A_j}=W$.

Since $T_\Gamma$ has $|U_\Gamma|-1$ edges, it requires at most
$|U_\Gamma|-1$ connector variables. By
\eqref{eq:core-cluster-data},
$U_\Gamma=\bigcup_{i=1}^{\ell}\gamma_i$ and
$K_\Gamma=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}|\gamma_i|$, and therefore
$|U_\Gamma|\le K_\Gamma$. Hence the total number of variables added to
$W$ is at most
\[
 \sum_{\Gamma\in\mathcal E_Q}K_\Gamma\zeta_\Gamma=T,
\]
which proves the claimed bound $|W|+T$. If $|U_\Gamma|=1$, no connector
variable is needed.
<!-- end proof-36 -->
